4 Ways to Make Your Tree the Star of Your Holiday Decor

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Simple, time-saving tips for decking out the season’s main attraction.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your Christmas tree is the centerpiece of your holiday decor, setting the tone for a season of joyful moments and beloved traditions—so how do you make it spotlight-ready? With the help of Lowe’s, we’ve outlined four easy ways to get your tree looking extra-special, from a quick lighting hack to adding greenery, layering ornaments, and creating a conversation starter.

Avoid the hassle (and heavy burden) of cutting down and carrying a live tree home by picking a lifelike artificial tree that you can use year after year. Many even come pre-lit, saving you valuable untangling and stringing time. You can also select a tree that fits your home’s overall decor style.

If you love all things vintage, this Holiday Living 7.5-foot pine with multicolor LED lights inspires whimsy and nostalgia. For a timeless, classic look, go with this Holiday Living 7.5-foot Tyler pine, which resembles and feels like an evergreen—plus, it features twinkling, color-changing LED lights that’ll draw attention. This Holiday Living 7.5-foot Kensington pine fits well into any contemporary aesthetic with its sleeker silhouette and an option to switch between vibrant white and multicolor LED lights.

Holiday Living 7.5-ft Vintage Pine Artificial Christmas Tree

Holiday Living 7.5-ft Tyler Pine Artificial Christmas Tree

Holiday Living 7.5-ft New Kensington Pine Artificial Christmas Tree

Accentuate your tree by draping the areas around it with more greens—this will lead the eye toward it and make it feel even more like a focal point. Placing Holiday Living artificial cedar pine garlands across your mantel, along floating shelves and hutches, and around doorways will act as a visual frame for the tree. If your space is on the small side, simplify by hanging an artificial Holiday Living cedar wreath on either side of the tree, which provides the same effect without crowding the room.

Holiday Living 6-ft Artificial Real Feel Cedar Garland

Holiday Living 24-in Artificial Real Feel Cedar Wreath

Once your base decorations are set up, make your tree pop with pretty ornaments and sparkling lights. Add cheerful red and white shatterproof Holiday Living Christmas ornaments, placing them from larger sizes at the bottom to smaller at the top, for a balanced appearance. Then, weave warm white Holiday Living LED string lights among your garlands and wrap them around your wreaths to further highlight greenery and give the whole room a festive glow.

Holiday Living Warm White LED String Lights

There’s a certain holiday magic that happens when your guests and neighbors can look upon your tree in the window. Make the scene even more joyful by arranging a holiday tableau with seasonal figurines to tell a story, showcase your personality, and make a scene that onlookers gravitate toward. A three-foot animatronic Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer and three-foot animatronic Clarice the Reindeer, each with motion-activated lights, sounds, and movements, will bring out the child in everyone.

Consider grouping the reindeer with other cute winter-attired animals—like this 24-inch LED Bernedoodle or 30-inch LED goose—and dream up a seasonal activity for them to perform (this is a particularly fun way to get kids involved). You could line them up with sheets of music in a caroling formation, or place props like snowballs at their feet.

Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer Animatronic Decoration

Rudolph the Red-Nosed Reindeer Clarice Animatronic Decoration

Holiday Living LED Bernedoodle Christmas Decoration

Holiday Living 30-in LED Goose Decoration

How can I make my Christmas tree stand out?

There are plenty of easy ways to make your Christmas tree the star of your holiday setup. String it with lights for a warm, welcoming glow; dress it up with eye-catching ornaments; and layer in greenery garlands across mantels and shelves. Hang a wreath nearby and arrange festive figurines beneath the branches to create a holiday display that feels both polished and inviting.

What are the best ways to light a Christmas tree?

If you want to save time, the fastest way to light a Christmas tree is to choose one pre-strung with lights. There are many lifelike artificial options available, some with programmable multicolor and white lights so you can change your display to match your decor or party theme. If you prefer to string your own lights on your tree, plan to purchase about 100 lights per foot of tree to fully cover it.

What Christmas decorations can I find at Lowe’s?

Lowe’s carries hundreds of on-trend holiday decorations to suit every style—vintage, contemporary, classic, and beyond. It has large and small pre-lit trees to fit any room, string lights in various colors, ornaments that range from traditional to whimsical, and indoor decor items like seasonal pillows, stockings, holiday villages, figurines, wreaths and garlands, and tabletop accessories.

The Most Extravagant Horseback Trips in the World

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The horse girl is having a moment. Riding boots are officially trending. Kendall Jenner recently celebrated her 31st birthday with an equestrian-themed fête. And who could forget earlier this year in Paris, when Stella McCartney kicked off her winter 2026 show at a riding hall in the Bois du Boulogne by sending out 10 horses—five black, five white, all chic?

Fashion, it seems, has discovered the horse. But horses have never needed discovering: Gucci and Hermès built empires on saddles and tack long before equestrianism found its way back into the fashion conversation. It may be the lunar calendar’s Year of the Horse, but for some of us, every year is Year of the Horse. For those ready to graduate from dressing like a horse girl to living like one, here are some of the world’s most extraordinary horseback adventures, from Patagonian ridge lines to African safaris. Some of them are multi-day journeys across wild landscapes, and others allow for deep immersion into single properties that offer particularly exceptional horse experiences. Saddle up.

In the rolling pampas of Uruguay just inland from luxe-boho beach town José Ignacio, Estancia Vik offers horseback riding with a pretty spectacular dinner reservation. Guests saddle up on Criollo and Quarter horses alongside expert gauchos for an evening ride timed to the rising of the full moon. The ride begins at the stables before sunset, winds toward the José Ignacio River, and culminates in wine and a traditional Uruguayan dinner under the stars. The estancia also offers equine meditation, through which you can connect with the herd and experience just how intuitive these animals really are.

One of my personal favorite horseback experiences anywhere was in the vast, unending salt pans of the Makgadikgadi in Botswana. Here, at Natural Selection’s Jack’s Camp, guests visiting at certain times of year can ride alongside 30,000 zebras and wildebeest at the peak of their migration before the rainy season.

For experienced riders, Micato Safaris offers a nine-night “Riding the Wild Plains” equestrian safari through Nairobi, Laikipia, and the Maasai Mara. The idea is to experience Kenya’s wildlife from the saddle, crossing through conservancies alongside giraffes, elephants, and rhinos with little more than the sound of hooves beneath you. After two nights at the posh Hemingways Nairobi, guests head into the bush for a trip through some of the country’s top safari lodges. But you’re hardly roughing it. Think champagne breakfasts and sundowners overlooking the savanna.

Few travelers know Central Asia quite like explorer, author, and television presenter Alexandra Tolstoy. A relative of the novelist Leo Tolstoy, she has been traveling the region since the 1990s, when she and three friends embarked on a nine-month, 5,000-mile journey by horseback and camel along the Silk Road, from Turkmenistan to China. More than 25 years later, Kyrgyzstan remains her great love. Tolstoy leads journeys through the country’s dramatic Tien Shan Mountains, drawing on decades of relationships with local horsemen, nomadic families, and mountain communities.

“Kyrgyzstan is, quite simply, one of the most extraordinary and beautiful countries in the world to explore on horseback,” Tolstoy told us. “Horses are deeply woven into the country's history, identity, and everyday life. This is a place where horsemanship is not a recreational pursuit but an integral part of the culture, and that makes the experience particularly special.” Not ready to commit to a trip? Check out Tolstoy Dacha, her line of beautiful objects for home and travel.

Many come for the skiing and hiking, but real horse lovers come for the riding. Above the town of Merano, just steps away from Miramonti Boutique Hotel, the Sulfner riding stable is home to the region’s distinctive flaxen-maned Haflinger horses, with guided excursions, riding lessons, and carriage rides available. After a day in the saddle, there’s an outdoor Japanese-inspired onsen pool and forest sauna to look forward to.

For experienced horseback riders, Tswalu Kalahari Reserve offers a different way to explore the largest private reserve in South Africa—by riding across grass-covered plains, over rolling red dunes, and over seasonal riverbeds. With a stable of breeds including Thoroughbreds, Arabians, Appaloosas, and English Shires, Tswalu’s horseback safaris bring adventurous travelers closer to the landscape and wildlife, offering an immersive perspective beyond the traditional game drive.

Chile offers a compelling case for exploring on horseback. This neck of the continent is known for excellent hiking, yes, but horseback allows you to cover much more ground in a single trip. Luxury travel company Red Savannah offers a 10-night riding “Chilean Riding Adventure” that begins in the Atacama Desert, where guests ride across the otherworldly sands of Moon Valley before spending evenings stargazing under some of the clearest skies on Earth. The trip continues on to the Lake District and ultimately down to Patagonia, where you’ll canter along remote lagoons beneath the watch of Andean condors. You’ll stay in luxe lodges from AndBeyond and Explora along the way.

In the heart of France’s Camargue, where wild marshlands meet the Mediterranean, horses are as much a part of the landscape as the salt flats and flamingos. At Les Bains Gardiens, a countryside retreat with its own stables, guests can saddle up for guided two-hour rides through the wetlands and out to the beach. Experienced riders can even gallop along the shoreline. The estate is home to traditional Camargue horses and often stages “jeux gardians,” the horseback games that celebrate this region’s centuries-old riding culture.

Archaeologists Found 2,500-Year-Old Battle Lines From a Conflict No One Knew Ever Happened

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Iron Age palisade likely blocked an invading army. But who built it—and who were they afraid of?

Archaeologists sifting through portions of Denmark’s western peninsula ahead of a highway expansion keep uncovering remnants of the area’s past, often in small snapshots: a grave here, an axe there, or maybe sometimes even the foundation of an ancient home. They certainly weren’t expecting to find the remains of battle lines in West Jutland, indicating a conflict they didn’t know ever transpired.

“The find came as a complete surprise to us,” Michael Alrø Jensen, curator at Museum Vest, said in a translated statement. “It points to previously unknown conflicts in West Jutland dating back to the earliest part of the Iron Age.”

The find showed up via a 2,500-year-old line of pits, which experts believe fighters created during the Iron Age to block hostile incursions along an ancient West Jutland thoroughfare. The discovery came as archaeologists from the museum examined fields along Route 11, between Varde and Korskro, before the Danish Road Directorate began a highway expansion.

Experts dubbed the battle lines, which were found beneath a field near Schaefergården, a “post-hole alignment,” likely from around 500 B.C.E. The lines feature a long series of closely spaced holes in which one end of pointed wooden posts were buried to form a defensive palisade.

“Our best theory is that there was a fear of an invading army arriving from the north via the ‘Drivvej’ route, and that attempts were therefore made to control and block the passage,” Jensen said.

More evidence may still lie buried at the site. Experts note that the current Route 11 alignment between Varde and Korskro matches the ancient “Drivvej,” or Drove Road, that was the main transportation line through West Jutland for centuries. The modern road project adds an additional lane to the two-lane highway and shows how closely the ancient road—whose origin date remains unknown—matches the modern setup.

Still, the find raises questions. Archaeologists and historians don’t know who developed the defensive structure—or why. The team hopes that additional work in the area may offer fresh clues about the ancient mystery.

Attempts to pinpoint answers to a 2,500-year-old question got a bit muddy as the excavations revealed artifacts from a range of eras in spots that hadn’t been searched previously. Archaeologists now have evidence of human activity along the road from the first Stone Age farmers, who buried their dead in small mounds through the Bronze Age, Iron Age, and into the Viking Age (including the discovery of a 4,500-year-old battle axe buried alongside a Stone Age warrior).

“What we are most excited about is that we have found traces from so many different periods within a relatively small area,” Jensen said. “We have suddenly gained a wealth of new pieces that provide much greater insight into how people lived and moved about in this region over several thousand years.”

Every new discovery provides nuance to the historical record, whether the remains of a house, farm, or grave. Museum Vest hopes to learn more by excavating additional burial mounds. The various discoveries in the region together form a narrative of humans’ transformation of the land, first into farming plots and then into villages. “Once our work is complete, we hope to be able to offer the local community a more cohesive narrative about the area they live in,” Jensen said.

Tim Newcomb is a journalist based in the Pacific Northwest. He covers stadiums, sneakers, gear, infrastructure, and more for a variety of publications, including Popular Mechanics. His favorite interviews have included sit-downs with Roger Federer in Switzerland, Kobe Bryant in Los Angeles, and Tinker Hatfield in Portland.

Speech Processing Pioneer Bishnu Saroop Atal Dies at 93

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

IEEE also bids farewell to innovators in space, power, and telecom

Bishnu Saroop Atal received the 2013 IEEE Jack S. Kilby Signal Processing Medal for “fundamental and pioneering contributions to speech coding.”

Bishnu Saroop AtalAcoustics and signal processing pioneerLife Fellow, 93; died 19 August

Atal was an acoustics and signal processing researcher at Bell Labs in Murray Hill, N.J., and later at AT&T’s Shannon Laboratory, in Florham Park, N.J. His pioneering work on voice-coding techniques became the foundation for efficient, high-quality voice transmission over cellular networks. He received the 2013 IEEE Jack S. Kilby Signal Processing Medal for “fundamental and pioneering contributions to speech coding.”

He joined Bell Labs in 1961 as a communications researcher, and his early work there focused on speaker identification technology. During the 1960s he helped develop linear predictive coding, a powerful method for analyzing and compressing speech. LPC is now the most widely used method for encoding speech at a low bit rate.

Atal developed increasingly sophisticated low-bit-rate speech-coding techniques. They included multipulse LPC, which improved the quality of compressed speech.

He also worked with his colleague Manfred R. Schroeder to develop code-excited linear prediction, a method for producing high-quality speech at very low bit rates. CELP became a foundational technique in modern speech coding, letting digital communications systems carry voice using less bandwidth. The extra efficiency expanded cellular capacity, allowing wireless networks to handle more simultaneous calls.

Later in his career, he led the acoustics and speech research departments at Bell.

The company named him a Fellow in 1994. When AT&T Bell Laboratories split into separate research organizations in 1996, Atal continued his career at the AT&T Florham Park headquarters, and he was named a Fellow the following year.

He retired in 2002 and became an affiliate professor of electrical engineering at the University of Washington, in Seattle. There he continued his research in speech processing, speech recognition, and high-dimensional computing.

Atal was elected to the National Academy of Engineering in 1987 and the National Academy of Sciences in 1993. He received numerous awards including the 2018 International Speech Communication Association Medal for Scientific Achievement and a 2003 medal from the Franklin Institute.

He received a bachelor’s degree in physics in 1952 from the University of Lucknow, in India, and a master’s degree in 1955 in electrical engineering from the Indian Institute of Science, in Bengaluru. While working full time at Bell he earned a Ph.D. in EE in 1968 from the Polytechnic Institute of Brooklyn (now New York University’s Tandon engineering school), in New York City.

Darryl Palmer Jr.Chair of the IEEE Central Illinois SectionSenior member, 55; died 30 August

Palmer was a dedicated IEEE volunteer, serving as chair of the Central Illinois Section and as the government relations chair of Region 4. He was a member of the IEEE Industrial Electronics Society and its industrial activity committee. He also served as associate editor of the IEEE Transactions on Industrial Informatics.

In 1986, at age 16, Palmer completed a summer internship at NASA’s Glenn Research Center, in Cleveland. He helped develop FoilSim, an interactive educational software program that introduces aerodynamic engineering to preuniversity students.

While studying computer science at Cleveland State University in 1996, he joined the university’s Allen-Bradley PLC lab as a research assistant. He designed hardware and software systems for the Glenn Research Center to collect and process thermal wave imaging data. He also designed software for General Motors’ North American robotic and welding division.

In 1999 he completed a second internship at the research center and helped develop EngineSim, an educational software program that models jet engine design and testing.

For his work, NASA recognized him with a Space Act Award.

In 2000 he earned his bachelor’s degree in computer science and became a software engineer at the Danfoss Drives manufacturing facility in Loves Park, Ill. He worked on embedded control systems for variable-frequency drives and high-power equipment. He also developed sensorless control technology for beam pumps used in the oil and gas industry.

In 2014 Palmer and his wife, Alexandra “Sasha” Goodwin, founded Janus Choice, a health technology platform that matches hospital patients with post-acute care facilities. He served as its CEO until the company closed in 2020.

From 2018 to 2023, he was a lead software engineer at Adept Coalescence, a startup in Rockford, Ill., focused on developing front-end software for control systems. He then worked in Peoria, Ill., as a control systems team lead at Caterpillar, a manufacturer of construction and mining equipment.

Henry Leopold RichterTelecommunications engineerLife senior member, 99; died 12 August

Richter was a spacecraft engineer at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory, in Pasadena, Calif., before starting his own public safety communications consultancy.

He earned a bachelor’s degree and a Ph.D. at Caltech in 1952 and 1956, respectively. In 1955 he joined JPL as an engineer and supervisor of its new circuit elements group. Later, he was assigned to NASA’s Explorer program as project manager for satellite design, serving as a liaison between the organization’s satellite instrumentation group and its operations and data groups. Since 1958 the program has launched more than 90 missions for physics, geophysics, and astrophysics research.

As NASA prepared to launch Explorer 1 in January 1958, Richter worked with the San Gabriel Valley Radio Club in Los Angeles to set up stations across the United States to track the satellite.

He eventually was named JPL section chief and was responsible for scientific instruments on the lunar Ranger, Surveyor, and Mariner programs. He also helped develop NASA’s Deep Space Network, an international tracking and communications system.

Richter left NASA to become vice president and technical director of Xerox Electro-Optical Systems, in Pasadena, Calif.

In the mid-1970s, he joined the Los Angeles County Sheriff’s Department as a communications engineer, overseeing the department’s microwave and mobile radio networks.

He later ran a telecommunications consulting practice for more than 20 years. His clients included federal, state, and local government agencies, school districts, universities, and organizations. His work covered strategic technology planning and the design and implementation of public safety radio, microwave, CCTV, and paging systems.

He wrote several books including America’s Leap Into Space: My Time at JPL and the First Explorer Satellites (2015), a personal account of his experience working on the Explorer 1 and America’s space program.

In 2019 the Radio Club of America presented him with its Lifetime Achievement Award for “significant achievements and a major body of work that has advanced the art and science of wireless technology.”

Raymond L. GiordanoFormer RCA engineerLife member, 91; died 19 July

Giordano was an electrical engineer who designed integrated circuits and defense electronics at companies including Harris, Intersil, and RCA.

After earning his bachelor’s degree in electrical engineering in 1956 from the Newark College of Engineering (now the New Jersey Institute of Technology), Giordano joined RCA in Moorestown, N.J., as an engineer. He helped develop a video formatter for the company’s missile and surface radar division, and he taught in-house engineering courses, eventually attaining the rank of senior technical staff member.

Later he developed technologies for General Electric (which acquired RCA in 1986), Intersil, and Harris. He designed the circuitry for Kodak’s Cameo cameras and one of its laser printers.

Giordano was awarded several patents for his designs including a smoke detector chip for Siemens, a speed control chip for Ford, an engine control system for Delco Remy, and a control component for Chrysler.

He earned a master’s degree in EE in 1964 from the University of Pennsylvania, in Philadelphia.

Delano D. WilsonPresident of Power TechnologiesLife Fellow, 92; died 26 May

Wilson was president and CEO of Power Technologies, a consulting firm in Schenectady, N.Y., that specialized in electric power systems.

He was a director of R&D at General Electric in Schenectady until he joined Power Technologies in 1974 as a principal engineer. He developed utility systems and software for the company.

Wilson authored six books and 65 technical papers, and he held two patents.

Outside of his engineering work, he served on the boards of several hospitals, YMCAs, Rotary clubs, and chambers of commerce.

After returning to his hometown of Great Falls, Mont., in 1995, he volunteered as a court-appointed advocate, a trained community volunteer appointed by a judge to represent the interests of children in foster care.

Daniel I. Amey Jr.Electronic packaging engineerLife Fellow, 84; died 12 March

Amey was an electronic packaging engineer, working at several companies including General Dynamics, Sperry Univac, and E.I. du Pont de Nemours (now DuPont). His research interests included connectors, chip packages, and high-frequency materials.

He began his career in 1963 as an engineer in the General Dynamics electronics division, where he designed military electronic hardware including analog and digital support equipment for aerospace applications.

In 1967 he joined Sperry Univac in Blue Bell, Pa. From 1972 to 1981 he was engineering manager of packaging techniques, responsible for interconnection hardware for small and medium computer systems. His work focused on large-scale integration packages, hybrid circuits, connectors, and printed wiring board processes.

He left the company to serve as director of electronics technology at Thomas & Betts of Raritan, N.J. He led research and development of packaging and interconnection products there.

In 1986 he became a director of Elco, an electrical component manufacturer in Huntingdon, Pa., where he was responsible for the design and development of connector and packaging hardware.

In 1988 he joined E.I. du Pont de Nemours as director of its electronics and communications technology group in Research Triangle Park, N.C. He worked on packaging technology development, high-frequency materials characterization, and applications engineering. He retired from there as a research fellow.

Amey wrote or cowrote more than 70 technical papers and held more than 14 patents. He developed courses on electronic packaging and printed wiring technology for education companies McGraw-Hill and Cobblestone (formerly the Center for Professional Advancement).

He received the International Microelectronics Assembly and Packaging Society’s 2004 Wagnon Technical Achievement Award and the Electronic Industries Association’s 1984 Distinguished Engineering Contribution Award.

After receiving a bachelor’s degree in electrical engineering and a master’s degree in engineering science from Pennsylvania State University, he earned an MBA from Lehigh University, in Bethlehem, Pa.

Amanda Davis is a freelance writer based in San Diego.

James Gunn’s brave and bold vision for the DCU deserved better than early grave

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get the biggest gaming news, reviews, and releases straight to your inbox.

Bringing all the latest movie news, features, and reviews to your inbox

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Is this the end of the DCU? Probably not, but it's a sad day all the same

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You'll have heard the news by now... As announced yesterday, James Gunn and Peter Safran are stepping away from DC Studios to be replaced by former Marvel exec Jeremy Latcham. The first phase of the DCU, it seems, is over. What comes next? Well, that's still very much TBC.

This is not entirely shocking news, but it’s still – for me at least – something of a downer. Gunn and Safran's DCU has been surprising, strange, daring, occasionally baffling, and often thrilling. I loved Superman and Lanterns and liked Peacemaker, Clayface, and Creature Commandos a great deal.

OK, the Supergirl film was a misfire and proved that Gunn's winning style was not so easily aped by other directors, but even that had its moments: Milly Alcock was terrific in the lead role, and Krypto is always fun.

The Marvel Cinematic Universe has been frequently criticised for the homogeneity of its output, but that was never a criticism that could be levelled at the DCU, which veered from family-friendly superhero flicks to rated-R horror movies with any given project.

That's probably not good news if you're a Warner Bros. exec desperately trying to forge the next big mega-franchise, however. And it's true that it has sometimes been hard to understand exactly what the criteria were for a DCU project making it to the screen or not. We exist in a strange timeline where we have a fairly solid Clayface movie, but no DCU Batman to go with it.

Still, when the DCU was good, it was great. Gunn's Superman managed the difficult task of delivering a fresh take on the Man of Steel. David Corenswet charmingly embodies Supes' decency, humor, and willingness to take a beating in a way that echoes Christopher Reeve's character-defining performance, but feels more in tune with the comics.

It helps that the supporting cast in that film – Rachel Brosnahan as Lois Lane, Edi Gathegi as Mister Terrific, and especially Nicholas Hoult as a scheming, raging Lex Luthor – were all pitch-perfect.

Then there was Lanterns. I will fully admit to being a die-hard fan of Damon Lindelof's work (The Leftovers is up there with the greatest shows of all time and I will never shut up about it) so I came to this with high expectations and was not let down.

Sure, some fans may have disagreed with the decision to squeeze the cosmic grandeur of the Green Lantern mythology into a self-consciously "grounded" cop show format, but it worked – and then some. Kyle Chandler's spiky, flawed Hal Jordan and Aaron Pierre's stoic, determined John Stewart were the dynamic duo we didn't know we needed, and their investigation into extraterrestrial weirdness in small-town America was both gripping and thought-provoking. This wasn't just a great comic book show, but excellent TV full stop.

As for the rest... I had a lot of fun with Clayface, a gloopy, gruesome, enjoyably schlocky horror that still found time for solid characterization. It's not the sort of film you can build a cinematic universe around, but that's OK. Peacemaker season 2, meanwhile, did not hit quite the same heights as that bolt-from-the-blue first run, but John Cena remains hilarious and heartbreaking in the role. I hope that the character isn't left stranded on Salvation for too long.

So what does a post-Gunn, post-Safran DCU look like? Obviously I'm purely speculating here, but I suspect this will be a case of Latcham refocusing the brand, rather than relaunching the entire universe for a third time. And no, I don't see Zack Snyder returning any time soon...

It doesn't take the World's Greatest Detective to figure out that we'll likely see a sharper focus on the A-list DC characters: Batman, Wonder Woman, and probably reinventions of Aquaman and the Flash at some point. I would hope that it doesn't mean the end of DC trying out interesting lower-budget experiments like Clayface, but I suspect the days of somewhat obscure-sounding TV spin-offs like the upcoming The People v. Gorilla Grodd are over.

The Superman movies will also surely continue, perhaps even with Gunn involved in a third instalment – while his departure letter talked of "trying" times behind-the-scenes, he notably did not close the door on working with DC again and has already referenced another, unnamed project in progress. Frankly, Latcham would be foolish to try and find someone else to steer that character when Gunn is right there.

James Gunn was working on Superman 3 and a Justice League team-up when he was ousted at DC

The biggest question, of course, is what becomes of the Dark Knight – and that does feel like a particularly tricky knot to undo. Matt Reeves' The Batman: Part II (which is set in a different "Elseworlds" continuity) was originally slated to come out in 2025, leaving the way clear for the introduction of a new DCU Batman in a film, helmed by Gunn, titled The Brave and the Bold.

Alas, The Batman: Part II suffered a series of major delays and is now eyeing a February 2028 slot, while the fate of The Brave and the Bold is unclear at this time. Ultimately, the DCU needs to find its Caped Crusader sooner rather than later, and you can be sure that figuring out how to introduce this new Batman, while also promoting Reeves' project, will be top of Latcham's to-do list at DC.

I'm not ready to write the future of the DCU off just yet, but I will miss the unpredictable energy that Gunn and Safran have brought to these movies and shows over the last four years. It's been a bumpy ride at times, but never a boring one, and I'd take a series with a strong authorial voice like this over a play-it-safe franchise any day.

Is that what we'll get with the DCU Chapter Two? Only time and a former Marvel exec named Jeremy Latcham will tell.

Clayface is in theaters now. The future may be in flux, but here's everything we know right now about DCU Chapter One and all the upcoming DC movies and shows.

Will Salmon is the former Streaming Editor for GamesRadar+. He has been writing about film, TV, comics, and music for more than 15 years, which is quite a long time if you stop and think about it. At Future he launched the scary movie magazine Horrorville, relaunched Comic Heroes, and has written for every issue of SFX magazine for well over a decade. His music writing has appeared in The Quietus, MOJO, Electronic Sound, Clash, and loads of other places too.

I Made a Mistake That Cost Me Everything. Rebuilding My Life and Career Taught Me These 2 Essential Leadership Lessons.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

What does it take to rebuild after your worst decision?

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

I know what it’s like to have people judge you by the worst decision you’ve ever made. When I was younger, I made a mistake that followed me for years. It ended up costing me opportunities, changing the direction of my life and eventually shaping the leader I am today.

The truth is, I was 20 years old and naive. I saw an underground, unregulated industry doing things wrong and thought I could do it better, which is how I began running a gray-market peptide and steroid company.

Even though I introduced strict operational standards, like batch-testing, the reality was that it was an illicit industry. The business was shut down; I was handed a federal indictment and eventually received a felony conviction. I lost everything.

I took full responsibility, paid a heavy price and spent the best part of the next decade rebuilding my life and career. What I’ve realized is that the experience changed how I think about myself and how I treat others. I know exactly what it costs to cut corners, and I know what it means to need a second chance.

After my conviction, banks wouldn’t touch me, and institutions flagged me. I was left with a choice: I could either let my past define me or spend the rest of my life proving I was better than my worst decision.

When I began building new companies, I knew they had to operate to the highest standards. Because of my past, being “good enough” was never an option. I had to prove I was doing things the right way — that my businesses were not only legitimate but could also be successful.

I’ve now spent more than 12 years operating businesses in highly regulated medical and telehealth sectors. I expect everyone I work with to meet those same high standards. But accountability has to start with me. As a founder, I can’t expect my employees to take responsibility for their decisions if I’m not prepared to do the same with my own.

I also had to give myself a second chance. I refused to wallow in shame and failure or let the fear of being judged stop me from sticking my neck out again. I backed myself. I know firsthand how important it is to get another chance. But I also believe people have to take responsibility for what’s gone wrong.

The line between giving someone another chance and holding them accountable comes down to two things: effort and ethics. There’s a difference between someone who’s struggling with a particular job and someone who simply isn’t willing to put the effort in. If they’re trying and willing to learn, I want to support them.

But accountability is non-negotiable. If someone violates our ethical standards, that’s an immediate dealbreaker. I know that might sound harsh coming from someone who’s needed a second chance himself. I also know that getting a second chance doesn’t mean escaping the consequences of your actions. I had to accept mine before I could rebuild.

People can change. I’ve had to prove that myself. But I can’t ignore a serious breach of trust just because I believe someone deserves another chance. My advice to other leaders is to ask whether the problem is skills, effort or integrity. They’re different problems and require different responses.

If someone has the right attitude, the raw hustle and that chip on their shoulder, I want to figure out where they fit rather than simply letting them go. I call this finding the right seat on the bus for the right people.

That’s also why I hire people based on who they are, their character and potential, rather than just qualifications or professional accolades. I particularly value people who have overcome adversity. That’s a kind of determination that can’t be taught.

I employ hundreds of people, and I take their livelihoods personally. Their families depend on them, and I believe loyalty has to work both ways. Some employees who joined my businesses a decade ago are still working with me today. Seeing members of my team buy homes and support their families is one of the most rewarding parts of what I do.

I didn’t want my belief in second chances to stop with my own employees. I had grit, determination and some luck on my side when I was rebuilding my life and career after my felony conviction. But I know there are plenty of people with the same determination who don’t get the opportunity to prove themselves afterwards. I feel like it’s my responsibility to change that.

My faith has also shaped my philosophy about second chances. I believe that people can take responsibility for their past and still deserve the opportunity to build a better future. That’s why I established the Joey Stiver Foundation to support educational, entrepreneurial and second-chance opportunities.

One of its initiatives, the St. Dismas Accelerator, aims to give formerly incarcerated adults and at-risk young people access to seed capital, executive mentorship and AI training. I’ve met people who’ve been through incredibly difficult experiences and come out of them with the kind of resourcefulness and determination you need to build a business.

I know some people won’t agree with my approach. They’ll wonder how I can have such strict standards while also believing so strongly in second chances. After everything I’ve experienced, I think both are important. When you’ve been through what I have, you realize you can fail, take responsibility, get back up and build again.

I’ve met plenty of people who’ve experienced failure or hardship and started to believe that success is something that happens to other people. But if you’ve got the drive, grit and determination to build something better for yourself, why shouldn’t you get that opportunity?

It’s something I remind myself, my team and anyone else who needs a reason to keep going. Why not you? The race isn’t over.

I know what it’s like to have people judge you by the worst decision you’ve ever made. When I was younger, I made a mistake that followed me for years. It ended up costing me opportunities, changing the direction of my life and eventually shaping the leader I am today.

The truth is, I was 20 years old and naive. I saw an underground, unregulated industry doing things wrong and thought I could do it better, which is how I began running a gray-market peptide and steroid company.

Even though I introduced strict operational standards, like batch-testing, the reality was that it was an illicit industry. The business was shut down; I was handed a federal indictment and eventually received a felony conviction. I lost everything.

US Bitcoin ETFs Lose $201M as BlackRock Withdrawals Extend the Slump

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

US-listed spot Bitcoin funds recorded another difficult session on Monday, November 9. The updated Farside Investors flow table shows $201.0 million in net outflows across the group, extending a three-session losing streak. The important detail is that the negative total was not evenly spread across issuers: BlackRock’s IBIT recorded $213.2 million leaving, while Morgan Stanley’s MSBT attracted $22.1 million.

TL;DR: Spot Bitcoin ETFs lost a net $201.0 million on November 9, following $430.2 million on November 5 and $411.9 million on November 6. BlackRock drove Monday’s withdrawals, while one smaller fund reported inflows. The figures measure fund creations and redemptions, not the intentions of every Bitcoin investor.

The latest fully reported Farside figures put the consecutive negative sessions at roughly $1.04 billion in aggregate. Thursday’s $430.2 million loss was followed by Friday’s $411.9 million and then Monday’s $201.0 million. That progression shows persistent selling pressure, although Monday’s net outflow was smaller than either of the previous two sessions.

Our previous report on Friday’s fund activity documented a similar pattern of issuer concentration. Going back further, the November 2 breakdown shows why it is worth examining individual ETF lines rather than reading the aggregate as a single institutional vote.

On November 9, ARK Invest and 21Shares’ ARKB showed a $9.9 million outflow. The positive contribution from MSBT offset part of the selling elsewhere. Several other funds showed no daily net creation or redemption. That does not mean no shares changed hands on exchanges; secondary-market trading and flows into or out of a fund’s underlying Bitcoin exposure are different measures.

BlackRock’s fund is a large and closely watched route into Bitcoin for investors using traditional brokerage and portfolio systems. Its persistent redemptions can therefore affect the tone of market commentary disproportionately. But the identity of an ETF’s end investors is not published in a daily flow table, and a withdrawal cannot automatically be attributed to hedge funds, pensions or retail holders.

The distinction between net flows and assets under management matters as well. A fund’s reported assets can fall when Bitcoin’s price drops even without corresponding redemptions. Conversely, share creations can occur on a day when the asset price weakens. Analysts who blend these concepts can make demand appear stronger or weaker than it actually was.

The next test is whether the large-issuer selling eases over several completed reporting sessions. One positive day would interrupt the streak but would not, by itself, demonstrate that buyers have regained control. It is also worth watching whether inflows broaden across products or remain confined to a single issuer.

These numbers are current through the November 9 US trading session, not an estimate of November 10 while trading is still under way. A better measure of sentiment combines a completed ETF flow series with spot volume, derivatives positioning and the changing rates outlook. For now, the latest evidence is that listed-fund demand remains weak even as the scale of net redemptions has moderated from last week’s peaks.

GTA 6 on PS5 Is PS Store's Most Pre-Ordered Digital Game in History

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It was always going to be big, but with a week until release, Rockstar has now confirmed that GTA 6 is the most pre-ordered digital game in PS Store history.

Confirmation comes courtesy of the PS Blog, in a post from Sony’s Tim Turi.

“GTA 6 is now the most pre-ordered game in the history of PS Store. Given this unprecedented demand, we’ve joined with Rockstar Games to allow players to start pre-loading the game nine days early, so they’re ready to play on launch day. We’ve also adopted a rolling midnight launch by country, beginning at 12:00AM NZDT in New Zealand and running across the globe as respective countries hit midnight on 19th November.”

You can learn more about when you’ll be able to play in our dedicated GTA 6 release times guide, so keep that bookmarked for 19th November.

Digital pre-orders for GTA 6 have been available since 25th June, so Rockstar has had a long time to build up numbers.

It’s worth reiterating that physical versions are following a “code-in-box” format, so there’s less incentive to purchase at retail, too.

(Although in a recent Push Square poll, a remarkable 23% of our readers did say they’d be plumping up for the boxed version.)

Record-breaking pre-orders like this are significant as the PS Store has been selling digital games for over a decade now; that it’s set a new record with over a week to spare and hype still building is remarkable.

While we don’t have actual numbers, Rockstar’s said they’re “exceeding” its external expectations.

Analyst firm Sensor Tower predicts 9.69 million pre-orders have been placed, with an astonishing 75% of those sales commanded by the PS5 version.

It’s worth noting that this milestone comes at a time of deep unrest among PS5 players, who’ve continuously spammed the platform holder with proclamations of ‘No Disc, No Buy’ in the aftermath of its decision to stop shipping physical games in 2028.

Sony recently reported year-over-year increases to its monthly active users, during a period in which enthusiasts mobilised a “blackout” of its platform.

Irrespective of fan frustration, it looks like Rockstar’s latest open world is days away from registering the biggest digital launch in industry history.

Talking Point: With Big Walk, The AI-In-Game-Dev Debate Just Got A Lot Murkier

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

"The line must be drawn here! This far, no further!"

Please forgive the Captain Picard First Contact quote (it's been a couple of weeks since Patrick Stewart's death and only now can I mention his most famous character without welling up) - I promise it's relevant as an illustration of an admirable, principled stance.

In the Borg, good ol' Jean-Luc had a well-defined enemy to engage, with the threat of humanity's assimilation providing a pretty clear line between good and bad. There are larger philosophical questions about the hive mind and our aversion to Star Trek's cube-riding automatons, but for the purposes of this article, let's assume we're all in agreement: Borg=baaad.

Back in the real world, though, that line between right and wrong often isn't so easy to define. Today there'll be a lot of commentators in the video game space, players and developers alike, longing for a better understanding of another encroaching, all-consuming entity.

House House's belated disclosure that LLMs were used during the development of indie hit Big Walk pours fuel on an already raging debate and brings questions over permissible AI use in game dev right back into the discourse - specifically the differences between generative AI for player-facing audio-visual assets and writing versus coding and other less obvious uses.

News Big Walk Devs Issue Statement Over AI Usage

This latest flashpoint followed a statement released by a subset of IGF Award jurors explaining why they won't be voting for games developed using any form of generative AI.

Last year, Clair Obscur: Expedition 33 — which swept The Game Awards with nine wins from 13 nominations — raised similar questions after the Indie Game Awards disqualified Sandfall Interactive's game upon discovering AI had been used in the development pipeline.

I've voiced my distaste for GenAI, and it remains a massive no-no - professionally and personally. I'm fortunate to not have corporate overlords mandating its use around these parts, which certainly isn't the case for some other outlets, nor for many creatives in the gaming space. The gulf in quality between the human-written word and generated bilge is still enormous and easily identifiable, and for now, the same is broadly true of GenAI visual artwork, too.

What this Big Walk situation highlights, however, is how difficult it can be to discern in other places, and the increasing pull it has on even the most principled developers.

Talking Point Does It Bother You That Big Games Companies Are Using GenAI?

The phrase gets bandied around a lot, but it's fair to call Big Walk a proper 'critical darling'. It garnered "Universal Acclaim" back in August, with an impressive 91 Metascore. Alex gave it a "Great" 8/10 after reviewing the Switch 2 version for us - technical hiccups took the shine off it for him, but he still loved the co-op experience. And by September it was already Nile's GOTY.

As someone who didn't play Big Walk at launch, its place in my GOTY-catch-up priorities may have shifted, but wherever you land on the GenAI spectrum, the consensus among critics and players is as unanimous as it gets: Big Walk is an excellent video game.

There are many issues to consider — not least where AI use crosses from tool to theft — but an important one relates to how this game in particular has had a profound effect on many players. Rejecting something flagged as 'suspicious' on principle is easy, especially with so many brilliant games releasing each week. But something you've played already and internalised and loved? From a dev team you respect?

For some, this revelation will feel like when a beloved artist/actor/writer/musician is revealed to be problematic, but we're not talking about illegal behaviour or allegations here.

If House House's statement is to be taken at face value, the team used a chatbot to find a solution to a coding issue with Unity, the third-party engine the game is built on. People online with real chops have already investigated the specifics of House House's single example:

took me 3 hours but I figured out the “URP culling shadowcasters behind the camera” issue House House cites in their disclosure. This culls immediately when the object is off-camera, but modifying the culling params with a custom frustrum is straightforward

this needs cleaning up but is basically the gist

This was a terrible use of 3 hours, but I know just a bit more about how to do something cool. I’m sympathetic to them wanting to ship something good but AI rots your damn brain

That's the one example the devs offer in their statement, and the language around further usage feels vague. The impression it gives is of a team searching for a solution to a specific problem using common tools, as opposed to vibe-coding huge swathes of the game.

Now, I've never programmed anything in my life, but I can appreciate that efficient code must be an art in itself. Is it any less of an art because that code is invisible to players? Then again, to me, generating great chunks of game code feels very different from using an LLM to squash a bug or find a solution when third-party documentation fails. Is one usage as 'bad' as the other, or is this slippery slope, gateway-drug territory?

(Perhaps if any programmers are reading, they'd like to share thoughts in the comments - as the headline says, this is very much a talking point and I'm keen to explore with as much nuance as the internet will permit.)

My point is that seeing exactly where to draw that line is increasingly difficult when AI tools of all types become ubiquitous. A blanket rejection of anything inevitably now means excluding not just an increasing number of games, but also artistically significant, important ones.

And with executives like former Nintendo of America president Doug Bowser advocating the use of AI for indie teams to improve workflow, it's also easy to understand how a developer might turn to the tech. The whole topic is getting greyer by the day, much to the pleasure of evangelists, I'm sure.

Like it or not, this situation forces AI sceptics to reconcile their positive feelings for the game. House House estimates "well under 0.1% of Big Walk’s code" has any trace of generative AI, but to paraphrase a certain Starfleet captain again, "How much does it take before it becomes wrong, hmm?" That is the question.

For some players who simply don't care, it's a non-issue. Some may reluctantly cut the game from their lives and GOTY lists. I suspect that others, though, disappointed or otherwise, will start reconsidering where they personally draw the AI line.

And if House House hadn't disclosed this, would anyone have ever known? I'd guess not. And if a celebrated indie team turned to AI as a problem-solver, how many other devs aren't disclosing similar usage? And how many sure-as-hell won't do so after this?

Lots of questions, no clear answers, just murk. How unsatisfying. I've heard 'ends-justify-means' sentiments online — 'hey, if the game is good, does it matter?' — which feel unsavoury given the documented, quantifiable damage AI is doing to the games industry and the wider world. It's that damage which has IGF jurors abstaining from voting for Big Walk this year: an understandable, admirable stance.

Which leads us to one final question: What would Captain Picard do?

Naturally, he'd call everyone into the Observation Lounge for a big old meeting in a calm, civilised manner, that's what. Conference.

Let us know below if this revelation changes your relationship with the game or where you draw the line when it comes to generative AI in its various forms.

We can't even have a meaningful discourse because people are so far entrenched in the tech being the worst thing since George Washington Carver chopped up George Washington, the best most flawless thing ever created, or they're just exhausted by both sides and don't care anymore.

Video games are an art. We want art made by humans.

However, coding is a different problem than drawings or music. First, as coders, we're always pushed to use the most efficient tools for the job. At work, I'm literally forced to use it. The company doesn't want to pay for the slow way of problem solving.

It causes as many problems as it solves in my opinion. The output code is now technical debt. But if it's used right, is it really that much different than leveraging a library to solve a problem? We don't ding developers for using Unity over assembly. Or using Lua over C#.

My honest opinion? I have a game and engine that I've been hand coding for over a decade, I'm not really up for losing ownership. I've enjoyed coding for over 30 years now and the game is my creative outlet. I refuse to use GenAI on it. But at the same time, you need to get off your high horse if you're really going to judge a dev for using it to solve one problem. That's just straight up offensive.

TL;DR: what the dev in the article did is nothing different from using stackoverflow and google, it’s a very good way to use AI.

I’m not a game dev but a software engineer with over 15 years of end-to-end more than just full stack software development experience who helped build up 2 successful startups with my own hand written code, no matter the coding language, backend, front end, infrastructure as code.

I’ve started to increasingly use AI because in today’s world it would burn you out to do anything without AI with all the pressure coming from needing to survive as a business.

My own rule is simply to use AI only to the capacity that ensures you have full control and AI doesn’t take over, and not in a way that replaces Human Resources but improves efficiency. In art this is difficult to achieve, but for coding it is simple.

1) If it’s some quick one-time tedious type of work (e.g. migrating to a new system), AI can be immensely helpful, you only need to review the end-result/output. I find the use of AI okay if you are capable of ensuring quality output and review it.

I had AI write scripts to do several types of migration work where it would take me weeks to learn all the tiny bits and unknowns, then write all the code. I used to do it all by hand and it is thankless work where you learn a lot of things you will never apply again in your life. Perfect scenario for AI to just do it. It can research everything needed and think of unknowns you couldn’t think of. Once the work is done, throw the scripts away because migration is done. Weeks of work reduced to a mere few hours. Instead I can focus on more fun parts of my job.

2) if its production level code that impacts user experience, I feel you should only use AI for assistance to improve and not to build. You need full control. We had an instance in the company somebody wrote the entire microservice via AI in a way he was incapable of debugging without AI. One part of it I had to rewrite completely by hand to fix it. They had no clue what they were doing and it could have been a disaster.

Unfortunately, I personally have come across too many lazy developers that make me prefer AI written code over their code, because AI simply codes better, more structured, can follow design patterns properly.

If you are unable to clearly envision the expected logic you intend to write, the expected design patterns used, don’t use AI. Learn first.

This brings me to 3) using AI to improve your skills is an extremely powerful tool. I told a friend to have AI teach him design patterns since I have no time for doing that, but he can always cross check with me. He has never improved so fast in his own hand-written creations before AI.

— Now I’ve tried using AI in various ways for coding and one thing for sure is that you need to be extremely good in giving boundaries and directions. Basically the correct “prompt”, and you can only do that if you have enough experience and skills yourself to control AI in that way. And you can never get there if you just let AI do all the work. It makes a lot of mistakes.

Now if I had to not worry about money, I would use AI far less and only for code reviews or learning, basically jsut as “feedback”, not a producer. But that’s not something I can afford, especially in a world where all employers now expect you to use AI to improve cost-efficiency.

Maybe not a common take, or a popular one, but I'm not a developer, yet I've been able to do amazing things with AI that I never could have without it. I have always had ideas and never been able to do anything with them because I lack the skill to code, and the time/desire to learn.

Since picking up Claude Code less than a year ago I've made several small games for myself that were terrible and I abandoned, but I've also been able to make some great stuff:

All of these projects are open source, on my github, and I will never charge for them, so I have the luxury of saying if you don't like it, don't use it. But I can sympathize with people who CAN code and are being forced to work faster not smarter on commercial projects, or losing their jobs altogether because 1 developer can do the work of 3, with the quality of none.

At work I've been able to (with my CEO's blessing) teach an AI agent how to fix 80% of the issues we see regularly to the point where over half of my daily work is done now with fully deterministic code, written by an AI agent, and the rest is triaged and investigated before I ever see it. Our time to resolution has gone down, and customer satisfaction has gone up, and we still send every communication by hand. None of the code is user facing, and none is supported by our dev team, so it's only to benefit our support staff.

I'm absolutely against using AI to replace people, whether it's in development, design, art, writing, communication, or anywhere else, but I'm a huge proponent of using it to enable your team to hand-off the time consuming, repetitive tasks to agents and focus on the things that the love about their job instead.

I'm very anti AI. I also turn my TV against the wall in case it steals my soul.

@Slowdive I think your blanket statement is at odds with the nuance the article is calling for.

"Do you use Google search? Then you are BAD. EVIL. NO GOOD."

See how ridiculous that sounds? I'm not pro-AI by any stretch but I do think we need to come to terms with the fact that technology will indeed advance, and AI tech is never going to go away. Let's call out bad USES of it instead! I think there's a lot of tools that are going to stick around that will enable tiny, small indie teams who use it PURPOSEFULLY to make some really lovely experiences.

One of the most common defenses of AI is that it's just a tool. This is, technically, true.

A hammer is also a tool. You can use a hammer to make a beautiful birdhouse. You can use it to make an ugly birdhouse. Or you can use it to bash in the brains of someone who criticized your birdhouse.

The difference is that there are already well established rules/laws in place about ethical hammer usage. With AI, we're really only getting started, and it's probably going to take a long time to figure out all the nuances, especially with a technology that will only continue to evolve.

I am also aware that there are environmental concerns, but I don't know enough to comment.

@OrangeSoda I mean, those laws haven't stopped hammers from being used wronfully.

Also, any actual illegal use of AI has already had people prosecuted. People generating pics that harm others have been jailed.

Of course when it's about copyright and whatever about protecting IPs, where it's a more grey issue about what is or isn't fair use, no one has faced actual consequences.

‘Not ready’, ‘out of control’ and ‘the end of the world’: What Amorim’s words tell us about Milan’s state

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ruben Amorim is not one to hold back when it comes to speaking his thoughts to the media, and the last few days are no exception.

The past week or so has seen Amorim have to digest the best and worst of his AC Milan side. The 3-1 derby win over Inter generated a euphoria that begun whispers of the word ‘Scudetto’, before a dismal defeat to Ferencvaros which saw Milan drop to 29th in the Europa League standings.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

This rollercoaster of extremes didn’t stop either side of those two games either. Before the victory over the Nerazzurri a 2-1 lead had been coughed up against Bologna, who scored three times in the second half to win at San Siro and inflict the first league defeat on the Rossoneri.

Yesterday, the Diavolo responded to the season low-point against the Hungarians by beating a Genoa side that had won their last three games and four of their last five. This Milan team is a very difficult one to work out, and it seems even the manager is struggling to do so.

After the Ferencvaros and Genoa games, we learned a lot about where Amorim sees his team to be at. Some key lines from his post-match comments demonstrated a wide spectrum of reaction, from anger to pride, passing through encouragement and worry.

Ruben Amorim has often gone to bat for his players after negative performances and results this season, but it seems that the defeat on Thursday night had pushed his patience to the brink as he tore into his team after the game.

“There’s no explanation. There’s nothing I can say, especially about the first half. I don’t remember another first half like it. It’s about ambition, concentration, desire,” he said.

“We gave away opportunities, we gave away stupid gifts. We weren’t prepared to play football tonight. I usually have a lot to say in defence of our team, but not tonight. We need to be more humble and understand what happened.”

For the first time we saw a peek behind the mask, one that has very often been one portraying an optimistic and positive expression. Amorim knew there was no point attempting to lie to the supporters, the media but above all to himself.

What we know is that Amorim echoed the ferocity of these sentiments when speaking in the dressing room after full-time in what was likely the first dressing down that the team got. Six players were reported to have disappointed more than the others, too.

Above all, what stood out was the fact that the head coach opted to question whether his players wanted it enough. This was no longer a question of learning an identity, rotation or an injury crisis: it became a matter of heart.

Another intriguing extract from the post-Ferencvaros tirade that Amorim went on was about the squad’s ability to balance playing in two different competitions, namely Serie A and the Europa League.

“We’re not ready to be competitive on two fronts. We need to work on our way of looking at football and how we approach matches. There are no excuses tonight, there’s nothing I can say.

“I have to focus on the league, obviously, but we also have to do well in the Europa League. We must and can win games despite the rotation. We have to be ready – we’re not now – to play and win every three days.

“But I won’t give up on this competition. But as long as the mathematics don’t condemn us, we’ll fight in this competition as we do in the others.”

Once again, Amorim offered a bit of a revelation: the league is the absolute priority for Milan this season. It may not exactly be revolutionary information, given that a top four spot remains the most accessible avenue to the main objective for 2026-27: securing a Champions League return.

And yet, in his emotional outburst, the former Sporting CP and Manchester United boss told us all in black and white that Serie A will be the focus. As a result, it’s right to expect more ups and downs in Europe, especially with such a depleted squad.

There is an element of observational realism about Amorim’s words; almost an admission of defeat in his attempts to spin two plates at the same time and get the most out of the resources at his disposal. Does it suggest a Europa League resurgence is coming? Not exactly.

Amorim has not made a habit of mentioning individuals ever since his arrivals, both in his pre-match words but especially after games in which emotions are running high. However, he brought up someone who was absent for the Ferencvaros game.

“Everyone needs to train like Pavlovic. He’s ready to die in training. He’s a good example. Sometimes when players don’t start, they struggle. But there are no excuses, we should have won even with those who started,” he said.

Going into the season there were some who were legitimately questioning whether Strahinja Pavlovic would even be a tactical fit for Amorim’s usual 3-4-2-1 system, given that he prefers more composed, ball-playing outside centre-backs.

The answer to those doubts is that in November the Serbian has become the shining light in the eyes of his manager. We mentioned earlier that it had become a question of heart, and nobody can doubt that Pavlovic has that in abundance.

For all of the talk about tactics, systems, shapes and patterns, football is a simple game that very often comes down to who wants it more. In this regard, Amorim elevated Pavlovic as the example to follow, while hoping he returns from injury for the Frosinone game after the break.

Moving on to the post-Genoa reflections, what was quite apparent was that Ruben Amorim was in no mood to get carried away. In fact, if anything, he revealed his belief that his team are still miles off where he wants them.

“What I feel is that, when we’re competitive, we’re difficult to beat, and now we’re steering the game towards our style. We push for control with the ball.

“When we’re not able to be competitive, we’re completely out of control. It’s a big difference, but it’s something we need to work on. But again, the team’s evolution, the way we’re playing, is completely different from the start of the season, and I’m happy with that.

“We should have more points. I’m clearly not happy with the European games, not just because of the results, but because of the performance. Results can happen, but the performances are far from what we want. However, I think we’ll be a better team in the future.”

So, for now this version of Milan is not one that satisfies the coach. The words are a good summary of what the last six games have shown, in which there have been an even split of victories and defeats: on their day the team can beat anyone in the league, but on an off-day they can lose to anyone too.

What Amorim seems keen to hammer home is that progress for this season will be judged on the ability to bring the ceiling closer to the floor. In other words, we have seen the potential of this team already with some impressive wins, yet the base level for performances remains frighteningly low.

Consistency is not just about being able to string together several positive results in a row, something the current Rossoneri do not seem capable of. It is about the repeated displaying of an idea and an identity every few days, to suggest the team are getting closer to the vision the coach has.

Various things make that difficult (perhaps unachievable, based on Amorim’s other comments) right now, such as the injury situation and the multiple windows needed to sculpt the squad. Progress is not always linear, but the target has been set.

One of the themes that Amorim has been consistently questioned on this season is how to unlock the club-record signing Goncalo Ramos, who remains stuck on three goals (one of which was a penalty).

After the Genoa game, he said: “I think we’re lacking time in the final third. We control the ball away from the opponent’s penalty area. This is also key for Goncalo Ramos to have more clear-cut chances. It’s clearly not Goncalo’s fault.

“Pulisic is far from the penalty area. Gonçalo is far from the penalty area. All these players play away from the penalty area, so I can say we’re still far from the final product, because we’re still working on good progression, but then in the final third of the pitch we waste a lot of time with the ball there.”

This is one of the points of ‘evolution’ that Amorim clearly wants to see from his side, but is yet to achieve. Milan are not yet a team that can sustain pressure in the opposition half, nor can they penetrate the box with any regularity either.

In fact, a report from Sky backed this up with some interesting numbers, look specifically at data per 30 minutes of actual possession. Milan are first in Serie A for build-up actions, but they are 20th for entries into the final third and entries into the opposition box.

Amorim’s side are 19th for percentage of touches in the attacking third and 18th in percentage of touches in the opposition box. When the Rossoneri have the ball, it is rarely in a threatening area.

With numbers like these, there isn’t much for Ramos to get on the end of, and it makes the positive attacking numbers (23 goals in 11 league games) feel quite unsustainable.

The Portuguese striker meanwhile is first in the Milan squad in Serie A for interceptions, recoveries, touches in the middle third and touches in the opposition half. He is second for receptions, touches and duels, and third for passes.

Simply put, the No.9 is being drawn away from the area he should be operating in because the team are not capable of sustaining spells of possession further up the field, so he needs to drop deeper to feel involved.

This is something Amorim must work on, because while the goals are coming from other sources for now – in some cases due to the space Ramos helps create – it could all come tumbling down unless the attacking play improves more generally.

🚨🎙️🛡️ Ruben Amorim on Gonçalo Ramos: “Ramos is helping US more than we are helping him. It’s a TEAM problem, not a Ramos problem.”

“We don’t spend enough time in the final third.”

“When we start keeping the ball there more often, Gonçalo is going to score MANY goals.” pic.twitter.com/b3Zs6YxNym

— Milan Wire (@MilanWireX) November 8, 2026

Finally, we end with what was perhaps the most passionate part of Amorim’s comments following the win at the Stadio Luigi Ferraris on Sunday, again centred around the levels he expects from his team.

“It’s the mentality. When you’re a player, there are no off days. If you play for Milan, for these clubs, there are no off days. You can’t show up like we did on Thursday and then turn it on again for Sunday. It’s not possible, it’s not something that will work.

“It’s not a physical condition, it’s a question of anxiety about defeat. If you ‘panic’ about defeat, it’s not that you’re afraid of losing, but that you know that losing is the end of the world. That’s the feeling I want my teams to have.”

In many ways, the manager was speaking in a way that channels how fans have experienced the past week. How is it possible that Milan can be completely outplayed by a side sat third in the Hungarian lead just days after convincingly beating the Italian champions?

Against Genoa, how can the team play as they did in the first half, and then appear to completely drop off a cliff in the second half when the time came to dig deep and bring the result home? The disparity in levels both in-game and from match to match is astounding everyone, even the man in charge.

What Amorim said about wanting his side to fear losing was particularly poignant. In his time at the club so far he has repeatedly stressed that feeling of responsibility to honour the red and black shirt and all that comes with that, including the importance of results.

Some may argue that encouraging an ‘end of the world’ type mentality will not help the players play more carefree and will actually only stiffen them up. Yet, it could also provide the stimulus for the fight and the courage that Amorim clearly so desperately wants.

There is a hint of irony about the ‘no days off’ comment, as Amorim has given the squad a one-week break, at least those who were not called up by their national teams. When everyone reconvenes there will be another crucial stretch of games to see out the year, starting with a home match against the surprise package Frosinone.

For all of the energy, passion and emotion that Ruben Amorim speaks with, it will eventually be time for words to become deeds. Consistency must be not a distant mirage but some thing tangible that emerges.

Only then will a full buy-in be achieved and patience be awarded. Otherwise there will always be the fear that darkness follows light.

Juventus Next Gen beaten 4-0 at Lecco and left third from bottom

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Juventus Next Gen were beaten 4-0 at Lecco in Serie C Girone A, with three of the goals coming after half-time. Coach Massimo Brambilla admitted his young side give goals away too easily, and the table now reflects a long poor run.

At the Stadio Rigamonti-Ceppi, the manner of the defeat was as worrying as the scoreline.

The opener set the tone. Lecco needed only six minutes to go ahead. Casarotto ran through the middle, got past Corradi and beat goalkeeper Mangiapoco. Lecco held that single-goal lead until half-time.

Next Gen did have one clear opening before the break. In the 40th minute, Grelaud shot from near the penalty spot. Vettorel turned the effort over for a corner.

The second half was one-way traffic. Capello made it 2-0 in the 50th minute, and Manetti added a third in the 56th. Capello struck from close range after the goalkeeper parried Pellegrino’s second attempt. Murillo’s goal in the 72nd minute summed up the match. Mangiapoco erred as Juventus built from the back, and Murillo pounced.

The hosts are in strong form. This was their second 4-0 win in a row, after beating Dolomiti Bellunesi by the same score. Lecco have now scored 15 goals in four games.

Massimo Brambilla did not hide from the problems. He told TuttoMercatoWeb that his side struggled against Lecco’s high press. His players found it hard to keep the ball against a team pressing hard and man-to-man.

The coach also admitted that coming back became difficult after the second goal. He said his players must improve how they read the game and stop giving away goals so easily.

The table makes the warning sharper. Brambilla’s side sit third from bottom after the defeat, having won just one of their last 12 matches.

The result also matters beyond Serie C. Carnevali has put the youth sector at the centre of the club’s plans.

One player supporters will watch is Destiny Elimoghale, who came off the bench at Lecco. Next Gen had started in a 3-5-2, with Durmisi and Merola in attack. Elimoghale replaced Durmisi in the 40th minute of the second half. The forward was born in 2009. His new contract, announced on 3 November, runs until 30 June 2029.

Elimoghale was called up to the first-team squad for the first time on 17 September 2026. That call-up came for the opening Europa League league-phase match against NEC.

The next opponents arrive in good spirits. Treviso beat Novara 2-0 in round 13. Romeo scored in the 24th minute, and Paganini added the second in the 59th.

The next test comes quickly. Next Gen host Treviso in round 14 on Sunday 15 November at 17:30. A trip to Giana Erminio follows on Saturday 21 November, at the same kick-off time.

Yes, this fits with Sony's broader pullback from PC, but the real reason the app is "retiring" is a simple lack of interest.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Sony took another step back from the PC today, announcing that the PlayStation Plus app for PC will be "retiring" on December 10. I can't really blame it for this one, though: Almost nobody was actually using it.

"PlayStation believes in providing high-quality experiences to our players, and we regularly review offerings to ensure we are focusing on experiences our members value most," Sony wrote at the tail-end of a PlayStation Blog update detailing the PlayStation Plus game lineup for November.

"With a very small number of eligible PlayStation Plus Premium members (less than 0.4%) using the PlayStation Plus app on PC, making this change allows us to focus on enhancing high-quality experiences that more of our members want to use. As a result, the PlayStation Plus cloud streaming app on PC will no longer be available."

The end of the app seems to fit pretty well with Sony's recent about-face on PC gaming: After a brief period of enthusiasm for bringing its big exclusives to PC, Sony began a walkback earlier this year, shifting its focus back to consoles exclusives. It hasn't explicitly acknowledged or explained the change in strategy, but as PC Gamer's Morgan Park wrote in May, the thing speaks for itself: Rather like Xbox "recommitting" to its console, "Sony is responding to a waning interest in consoles by reinforcing its 'narrative singleplayer games' as system sellers once again.

"Multiplayer games like the upcoming Marvel Tōkon will still come to PC, but if you want to play the next prestige action game from the likes of Naughty Dog or Insomniac, you'll need a PS5," Morgan wrote. Or, when the time comes, a PlayStation 6.

Similar to Xbox cloud streaming, the PlayStation Plus app on PC was really more noteworthy for the selection of older PlayStation games it offered to PC gamers, rather than major new releases. Given that, I think the end has come for exactly the reason Sony cited: Not due to any strategic realignment, but simply because nobody's using it in the first place.
