Un ultra, ce n'est pas un hooligan

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

So Foot, le magazine qui aborde le football sous l'angle de la culture, de la société, de la politique et de l'humour (selon la règle des « trois H » : humour, humain, histoire) publie une longue interview de Pascal Pacaly qui vient de sortir "Sainté Football Club", son dernier roman dans lequel il analyse 50 ans de sociologie footballistique à Saint-Étienne et en France, et fait vivre le foot, le sentir, le respirer à pleins poumons comme tout supporter passionné. Ce roman mêle évidemment histoire de l’ASSE, témoignages de proches et évolutions de la société française. Pascal Pacaly nous livre notamment sa vision du phénomène ultra et en vient à analyser les bouleversements actuels de la société :

Au sujet des procédures de dissolution des kops : "J’ai un peu vu ça comme de la communication. C’était pour montrer qu’ils faisaient des choses contre la violence, quoi. L’idée a été de dire que « les ultras, c’est des méchants, ils sont homophobes… » Commencez par changer les exemples de divisions que vous donnez, au lieu de taper bêtement sur le foot. Comme ce sont des mecs qui n’y connaissent pas grand-chose, ils font même le mélange avec des hooligans… alors qu’un ultra, c’est pas ça du tout. Tu touches à un sport populaire qui fait du lien entre les gens. Par exemple, les kops ont beaucoup récupéré des jeunes qui traînaient dehors pour leur donner des petites tâches avant les matchs, etc. Ça leur redonnait confiance et ils se sentaient utiles. On parle beaucoup des fumigènes et des bagarres, mais jamais de ce côté social qu’amènent les groupes."

Au sujet du "C'était mieux avant" : "Quand je regarde la société d’aujourd’hui, je trouve qu’il y a dix fois plus de haine et de violence qu’à mon époque. Même au niveau de la politique, les gens arrivaient à discuter même s’ils étaient pas du même bord. Aujourd’hui, soit t’es un gaucho soit t’es un facho. Y a pas de volonté de comprendre l’autre, on ne dialogue plus. Un parallèle avec le foot, je me rappelle d’Étienne Mendy, notre avant-centre dans les années 1990, un supporter à qui il avait signé un autographe lui a demandé de le déposer à la gare et il l’a fait. Ce sont des scènes que tu ne verras plus, parce que les clubs sont devenus des bunkers."

Sur les conséquences des réseaux sociaux : "Oui, ça génère trop de commentaires négatifs. Regarde Gautier Larsonneur, il y a eu un bashing de malade… Ok, il est pas bon mais c’est pas une raison pour s’acharner sur lui… Il y a plein de choses dans la vie qui peuvent affecter ton rendement. Des gens disaient qu’il traînait beaucoup dans les bars, peut-être que c’est vrai hein, mais d’autres l’ont fait avant lui, Alex Dias, Pascal Feindouno… Aujourd’hui, on ne te pardonne rien. On a perdu ce sens de la juste mesure, tout ça pour exister."

Court appears unlikely to side with inmate in lawsuit for violation of his constitutional rights

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Monday appeared to side with a prison paramedic in a lawsuit brought by an inmate who contends that the paramedic’s failure to provide medical treatment violated his constitutional rights. During just over 80 minutes of oral argument in Nielsen v. Watanabe, a majority of the justices seemed poised to throw out the inmate’s lawsuit, even if it wasn’t clear whether they were ready to bar such lawsuits more broadly in the future.

The inmate, Kekai Watanabe, was seriously injured in a gang-related fight while he was in a federal detention center in Hawaii. He says that he told Francis Nielsen, a paramedic who treated him, that his pain was severe, but he received only over-the-counter pain medication and was put in solitary detention, where he remained for more than two months without seeing a specialist or going to a hospital. When Watanabe received an X-ray seven months later, it revealed that he had a fractured coccyx and that chips from the bone had penetrated the soft tissue in the area. He says that he continues to be in “tremendous pain.”

Watanabe filed a lawsuit in federal court in Honolulu, alleging that Nielsen violated his rights under the Eighth Amendment to be free of cruel and unusual punishment by being deliberately indifferent to his serious medical needs. He relied on two Supreme Court cases: the court’s 1971 decision in Bivens v. Six Unknown Named Agents of Federal Bureau of Narcotics, holding that a violation of an individual’s constitutional rights by a federal official can create a federal claim for damages; and its 1980 decision in Carlson v. Green, in which the court held that the estate of an inmate who died after having an asthma attack could bring a Bivens claim when prison officials had failed to provide adequate medical care for eight hours after the attack and did not send him to the hospital during that time.

The district court dismissed Watanabe’s claim. It held that Carlson did not apply because his claim was not sufficiently similar to the facts of that case.

The U.S. Court of Appeals for the 9th Circuit reversed. It acknowledged that the Supreme Court “has cautioned against extending Bivens remedies to new contexts,” but explained that, in its view, “Watanabe’s claim is identical to Carlson in all meaningful respects” and therefore can proceed. A deeply divided court of appeals declined to rehear the case.

Representing Nielsen, Lucas Walker told the justices on Monday that the Supreme Court “has repeatedly explained that Bivens claims are disfavored, that they strain the separation of powers, and that they cannot proceed if there is any reason to think that Congress might be better suited to weigh the costs and benefits of a damages remedy.”

But Watanabe’s “claim ... also fails under a straightforward application of this Court’s Bivens framework,” Walker continued. “There are at least three” “meaningful difference[s] between his case and Carlson.” First, Watanabe had other options besides damages – such as seeking “administrative and injunctive relief.” Second, unlike the inmate in Carlson, Watanabe did not die, which “Congress could see [as] a meaningful difference.” Third and finally, there are “special factors” at play in Watanabe’s case that were not at play in Carlson, such as the Administrative Remedy Program, a grievance process created by the Bureau of Prisons in 1979, and federal laws enacted after the decision, such as the Prison Litigation Reform Act, a 1996 law that requires prisoners to use all available internal prison appeals like the ARP before going to court.

Vivek Suri, an assistant to the U.S. solicitor general who argued on behalf of the federal government, told the justices that, in the view of the United States, there are “three ways in which this Court could resolve the case.” “First, it could apply its existing standards and recognize that Carlson is now obsolete. Second, it could ... explicitly overrule Carlson. And, third, it could distinguish Carlson on its facts.” He “respectfully urge[d]” the court to choose one of the first two options, “either of which would abrogate Carlson, fully resolve the” divisions among the courts of appeals “that it has created, and end the severe practical problems that it creates for prison administration.”

Representing Watanabe, Joshua Wesneski reiterated that “the Court’s consistent message for Bivens and Carlson over the past decade” has been “[t]his far and no further.” “‘No further,’ he said, “means we should not extend those precedents. But ‘this far’ means also that Carlson remains settled law within its core context and should not be reduced to a null set. We ask only that the Court adhere to that approach.” “Indeed,” he suggested, “to overrule Carlson now would disregard Congress’s measured judgment in this area and eliminate a crucial bulwark against the encroachment on the constitutional rights of some of society’s most vulnerable members.”

Neither Chief Justice John Roberts nor Justice Brett Kavanaugh seemed receptive to Walker’s suggestion that the court should go ahead and overrule both Bivens and Carlson.

Kavanaugh asked Walker how the court should handle the “fact ... that Congress has had 55 years since Bivens and 46 years since Carlson to do so and has not done so?” “It seems,” Kavanaugh continued, “like Congress, in fact, has left Bivens and Carlson where they stood ... and Congress has operated against that backdrop.”

Walker suggested that the Supreme Court “has not hesitated to overrule past decisions, especially those where the courts had overstepped the proper judicial role.”

In response to Walker’s assertion that overruling Bivens would “clear up the confusion and hand the accountability for all of this back to Congress, where it belongs,” Kavanaugh later asked whether “another way to provide clarity [would be] to simply say that courts should apply Bivens and Carlson but not extend them?” He compared such a scenario to what the Supreme Court has done in other areas of the law, such as with the Alien Tort Statute, “where we’ve had results or decisions under a methodology we wouldn’t use anymore, but we have not overruled the original three” causes of action under “the ATS, for example. We just haven’t extended them.”

Suri addressed this point during his time at the lectern, telling Kavanaugh that “[t]he problem ... as I see it ... is the Court’s decision” holding that “the PLRA itself gives rise to a new context,” as does “the availability of injunctive relief.” “So,” he continued, “if you’re going to honor that precedent, then this is a new context,” which would mean that “there are no viable Carlson claims anymore.”

For his part, Roberts noted that the federal government had “expressly” not asked the court to overrule Bivens, “and, of course, they have broad experience in this context. And I wonder,” he asked Walker, “if you have any insight into why or at least why shouldn’t we, on that question, defer to their expertise and experience on that question?”

Walker responded that “I think the answer is that this has been an area wrought with confusion where the lower courts have really called out for guidance.”

Roberts appeared unconvinced. “[O]n the other hand,” he countered, “it could be viewed as a cautious, incremental approach to such a fundamental question” to leave Bivens intact for now.

Several justices focused on whether, if a Carlson claim was not available, an inmate in Watanabe’s shoes would still have some remedy available to him. Justice Neil Gorsuch explored whether an inmate who was the victim of medical malpractice would still be able to obtain relief in a lawsuit against the United States (rather than the individual prison official) under Federal Tort Claims Act, a 1946 law that allows private citizens to sue the federal government for (among other things) injuries caused by a federal employee.

Gorsuch elicited from Suri that “[i]n almost all the cases that” he had “encountered, ... there is an FTCA claim brought in parallel” with the claim under Carlson – in part, Suri suggested, because the standard for prevailing is lower, qualified immunity does not apply, and “[y]ou have a defendant who’s able to pay, the United States.” Suri suggested that when an inmate does not bring both kinds of claims, it may be out of a desire to harass an individual prison official or because the inmates do not have lawyers and are instead representing themselves “and are confused about how to proceed. If this Court says there are no more Carlson claims,” he concluded, “then I think it’s more likely that they will bring FTCA claims in these cases.”

Justice Samuel Alito had a similar question, asking Suri “whether Bivens in this context provides anything ... of value that is not available based on other remedies.”

Suri responded that Carlson had rested on the idea that “the FTCA was insufficient” because it “does not provide for punitive damages.” In Bivens, he emphasized, the court faced a choice between “damages or nothing” for the plaintiff. But under the FTCA, he said, “you’re already getting damages.”

Wesneski pushed back on any suggestion that the FTCA, standing alone, would – as Kavanaugh put it – “provide some remedy in meritorious cases.” The “principal virtue” of the FTCA, he stressed, “is the deterrent effect on the individual officer.” And there would be other potential problems as well, he posited: for example, he said, “we know the plaintiff in Carlson v. Green would not have had a cause of action under the FTCA” under Indiana law.

Justice Amy Coney Barrett also expressed concern about what relief an inmate in a situation like Watanabe’s would be able to obtain. She first pressed Walker on the question of whether an inmate could obtain a court order instructing a prison official to do (or to refrain from doing) something, even if he could not obtain money damages for the official’s past conduct. “I want to make sure,” she said to Walker, “that, in your position, there’s no doubt that someone in a prisoner’s position like this would be able to get injunctive relief against a federal officer.”

And she later returned to a similar topic, asking Walker to “walk me through what happens in a case where a prisoner dies as a result of medical negligence and let’s say really horrific medical negligence. What recourse,” she enquired, “does that prisoner have? Is it the FTCA?”

Walker assured Barrett that, even if Carlson were overruled, claims like the one she described would generally be allowed to go forward.

Justices Sonia Sotomayor and Ketanji Brown Jackson appeared to be the most likely to side with Watanabe. Sotomayor contended that, “in the Westfall Act, Congress looked at Bivens and explicitly reserved those constitutional claims the Court had recognized” – specifically, “Bivens itself for unlawful entry ... without probable cause into private citizens’ homes when there was malfeasance in doing so, and in Carlson itself, which was a ... medical malpractice claim.”

Walker resisted Sotomayor’s characterization, telling Sotomayor that, “respectfully, I don’t think the Court has ever gone so far as to say that the Westfall Act ratified Bivens or Carlson. But what the Court has said is that it does not provide any reason to extend those cases further.”

Jackson seemed to regard Watanabe’s case as, “with respect to all major indicia of sameness,” identical to Carlson. “We have the same kind of alleged violation as in Carlson,” she told Walker. “We have the same constitutional right brought under the same legal framework by the same kind of defendant who’s housed in the same kind of federal institution.”

By contrast, Justice Elena Kagan suggested that, at the very least, Watanabe’s case involves “factual differences.” In addition to the fact that the inmate in Carlson died, she noted that “there was a view that the prison officials had actively contributed to the injury, and ... that the prison officials knew exactly what was happening while it was happening.” “[A] different way of saying the same thing,” she added a few minutes later, “is that this looks like a pretty standard medical malpractice case, bad but standard, where the factual circumstances in Carlson look like something a good deal more than that.”

A decision in the case is expected by late June or early July.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

Barracuda’s email security gateways bitten by make-me-root vulns

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Security company Barracuda warns that attackers are exploiting a pre-authentication authorization bypass bug and a command-injection vulnerability in its popular Email Security Gateway (ESG) products that, when chained, could allow the crims to run commands with root privileges.

“We have identified exploitation activity affecting a small number of organizations,” the security vendor said in a late Monday night update to its earlier security advisory.

Barracuda declined to answer The Register’s questions, including one about how many organizations have been hit by attackers. A company spokesperson told us that it “worked directly” with the affected businesses “to quickly address the issue. Barracuda acted promptly to remediate the vulnerabilities, releasing patches for affected products.”

The vendor disclosed and fixed the vulnerabilities, CVE-2026-116606 and CVE-2026-111560, last week.

CVE-2026-116606 is a 9.3 CVSS-rated, pre-authentication authorization bypass flaw in ESG. While authentication is not required to exploit this vulnerability, the attacker must have access to the administrative user interface.

As Purplecop Security founder Tausif Ahmed said on Tuesday: “If your Email Security Gateway's admin UI is open to the internet, that's the first thing I'd close today.”

CVE-2026-111560 is an 8.6-rated command-injection flaw that affects multiple Barracuda products: its ESG, load balancer (ADC), message archiver (BMA), web application firewall (WAF), WAF control center (WCC), and web security gateway (WSG).

“If exploited in combination, these vulnerabilities could allow an attacker to bypass authorization controls and obtain root access to the device,” Barracuda warned on November 2. At the time, the vendor did not report any active exploitation of either.

We don’t yet know who is responsible for the attacks, or whether the intruders gained root access – but if they did, they could steal data, install backdoors, and take full control of affected systems.

These types of edge-device exploits have been used by Chinese-government spies, and in 2023 the FBI blamed PRC-linked attackers for a series of attacks that targeted Barracuda ESGs to deploy custom malware and maintain access to high-value networks. According to Mandiant, 27 percent of identified affected organizations were government entities.

5 Expert Habits to Find Inner Peace from Best-Selling Author Yung Pueblo

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

This is Expert Habits, a regular column in which thinkers, leaders, and other high performers talk about the habits and routines that help them live a better life.

Yung Pueblo wants to be clear on something: “I’m not a monk.” Sure, in his lifetime he’s meditated for over 13,000 hours, but he still enjoys the drama and chaos of Love Island UK like the rest of us. The best-selling author’s philosophical thoughts on self-awareness, emotional healing, and mindfulness have reached over 3.5 million followers since he first started posting to Instagram in 2013. His digital presence has since grown into published works of poetry and prose, including the New York Times bestsellers Inward, Clarity & Connection, and The Way Forward. Now Pueblo, whose real name is Diego Perez, is releasing a new book: Journey: The Year That Changes Everything (Penguin Random House), a 365-day guide to finding inner peace.

What exactly is inner peace to Perez? “It means that you have a good relationship with your emotions,” he says. “It’s that you’re able to feel your emotions without necessarily fueling the heavy ones.”

Inner peace, per Perez, isn’t a final destination. It’s about recognizing negative feelings and using tools you’ve learned to acknowledge that they’re happening without allowing them to take over your life. That’s what Journey—which debuts on November 10—is about. The following habits are just a few of the techniques he’s developed to do exactly that. Below, Perez explains the creative benefits of walking, the value of daily gratitude, and why you should protect your own peace even when others around you are chaotic—the latter of which makes keeping up with reality TV that much more enjoyable.

Yung Pueblo: “Oftentimes we have a relationship where we’re afraid of change and we need to realize that the universe that we live in, it’s totally founded on change at every level of existence—the atomic level, the biological level, the cosmological level. It’s because of change that we even have the opportunity to experience things. If our universe was static, we wouldn’t be having this conversation right now. So changing our relationship with impermanence and making it one where we actually get inspired to be more present. When we’re with our friends, with our family, with your mom, with your dad, you know that they’re not always going to be around. So the times when you are with them, to be ever more present, because you know change will happen at one point or another. Being able to have that presence just makes you have this ability to really enjoy some of the most beautiful parts of life.

“When it’s a sad moment, you have to be like, ‘OK, this is not going to last forever.’ But also in the precious moments, this isn’t going to last forever, so let me be here in the moment.

“Another way to look at it is that it’s defined by your relationship with change. So the more that you’re able to embrace change and flow with change, then your mind’s going to have less stress. But it’s probably one of the biggest challenges any human being can accept— willingly trying to change their relationship with change.”

“So much of our impulsiveness just comes from these lightning-quick reactions that we think are our nature. We think that they’re unchangeable, but the reality is that we can train ourselves to slow down to be able to ask: OK, is this me having an impulsive reaction right now? and then asking ourselves: Is this actually useful? Should I act this out or instead should I actually choose something that’s different than what I would’ve done in the past? And in the act of slowing down and being intentional, you just end up finding way better results. This is a real habit. You can learn that from meditating, you can learn that from therapy, but you can also just teach yourself the raw skill of feeling impulsiveness coming up and then challenging yourself to slow down.

“I’m not going to jump to conclusions. I’m not going to say anything right now. Let me just check in with how I feel, what actually feels true to me, and then put forward something that I think is actually going to be helpful as opposed to just something that I later regret.

“So if a friend of yours is telling you about something that they’re really mad about, they’re offering an invitation for you to be angry with them on their behalf. But instead I found that I don’t necessarily want to be angry, but I want to be able to hear my friends and offer support or a listening ear or whatever the situation is needed. At the same time, I can remain in my own energy while offering support to this individual. We get sucked into the energy of other people really, really quickly, but instead of getting sucked into someone’s chaos, for the past two years, what I’ve been working on is: Can I live in my peace and offer my peace as an invitation? They can join me in my peace too, if they want to, instead of me just getting sucked into their drama.”

“We fall into self-analysis when we don’t feel good. It’s just the worst time for you to be analyzing yourself about what you’ve done right, what you’ve done wrong. Negative self-talk isn’t productive. It’s good for you to be honest with yourself, but honesty also can come with gentleness and with self-acceptance. But when your mood is low, it’s going to be really hard for you to be gentle with yourself. So one thing I realize is when I don’t feel good or I’m over-exhausted from too much work or too much travel or whatnot, that’s the time for me to just suspend self-analysis and just treat myself gently—be mindful of what I’m saying to other people too, and just let it pass.

“And in the act of letting it pass, then you can do some more proper self-assessment because your best assessment is going to be when your mind is calm, not when it’s heavy.”

“Life is a long series of ups and downs, and there’s nothing wrong with that. But in the down moments, we tend to look at everything with such darkness that we forget that we still have access to clean water, that we still have access to food, that we have a house over our head. A lot of people in the world don’t have that. Having gratitude for the basics and the fundamentals really helps reset the balance where if something went wrong at work, you’re going to be okay, you’re going to be fine.”

“This is my recent favorite and it’s the newest habit that I picked up. You got to walk. You got to go for walks. Walking supports clarity, and I think it really supports creativity. I find that whenever I feel a little stuck with writing, just going on a 20- or 40-minute walk really helps to regenerate and create this new sense of clarity.”

Colorado nonprofit launches plug-in solar program ahead of new state law

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 10, 2026

A new Colorado nonprofit is looking to bring distributed solar to households that have traditionally been left out of the residential solar market.

Solar Access Colorado, which bills itself as the state’s first organization dedicated to plug-in and balcony solar systems, has launched with pre-orders for small-scale solar kits ahead of the implementation of Colorado House Bill 26-1007, also known as the Improve Customer Use of Distributed Resources bill. The legislation establishes consumer protections for qualifying portable-scale solar devices, including provisions intended to make adoption easier for renters and homeowners living in communities governed by homeowners associations.

The Denver-based organization is positioning plug-in solar as an alternative for residents who cannot install traditional rooftop systems because they rent, live in multifamily buildings or lack suitable roof space.

“Colorado has taken an important step toward expanding access to distributed solar energy,” said Adam Harris, founder of Solar Access Colorado. “We’re launching Solar Access Colorado to help renters, apartment residents and homeowners participate in the clean energy transition with practical, accessible solar solutions.”

HB26-1007 creates a framework for personal, portable-scale solar generation devices. Beginning Jan. 1, 2027, the law will protect qualifying systems from unreasonable restrictions on their installation, use and operation.

The legislation also addresses HOA restrictions by treating qualifying portable-scale solar devices as energy efficiency measures under Colorado’s common-interest-community laws. This could help expand access to solar for residents who previously faced restrictions on making property modifications.

However, the law does not eliminate all installation requirements. Reasonable safety-related restrictions remain permissible, including provisions addressing secure mounting, fire prevention, electrical overload and potential property damage.

Solar Access Colorado plans to help consumers understand the new regulatory landscape and navigate product requirements and installation considerations as the law takes effect. Its initial product offering is designed for balconies, patios, fences and other outdoor spaces, giving residents additional options for generating electricity without committing to a conventional rooftop installation.

Beyond product sales, Solar Access Colorado plans to work with municipalities, housing organizations, property managers and community groups to broaden access to plug-in solar. The nonprofit also intends to prioritize education and outreach to low-income communities and multifamily households.

As states explore policies supporting smaller, customer-sited generation systems, plug-in solar could become another entry point for residential clean energy adoption. Colorado’s upcoming legislation is providing a framework for that market to develop.

Palestra: ‘Italy defeat against Bosnia still hurts, but we have to turn the page’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Italy and Chelsea full-back Marco Palestra admits that the World Cup play-off final defeat against Bosnia and Herzegovina earlier this year ‘still hurts a lot’ and potentially ‘always will’, but says that it is now down to the players to turn the page and write a new chapter in the history books.

Palestra was called up to the Italy national team squad for the first time since Roberto Mancini was reinstated as CT last week and is hoping to put past disappointments behind him heading into Nations League fixtures against France and Belgium later this week.

The 21-year-old missed out on selection for the first international break of the season as he was recovering from a muscular injury he picked up with Chelsea just before the 2026-27 Premier League season got underway.

That injury has limited Palestra to just two Premier League starts since his €60m transfer from Atalanta over the summer, but the young Azzurri talent had begun to feature more regularly in Xabi Alonso’s side prior to the international break.

“It’s all been positive despite the injury and recovery period. I’ve started to play recently, I’m doing fine and feeling good,” said Palestra in a recent interview with Rai Sport (via TMW).

“I have to thank my coach and my teammates. I’m fortunate because they managed me well when I got back, bringing me in slowly and calmly, I’m feeling good now.”

Palestra insists that he is not concerned by the fact that he has not registered any goal involvements during his time with Chelsea thus far.

“No, definitely not. I don’t decide when I get to score. All I can do is try and play well and improve as much as possible. It doesn’t weigh on me too much. I’m focused on improving and playing as much as possible.

“I’m lucky to be in a group of strong players and a great coaching staff, who all help me. I’ve also come to the strongest league in the world, so it’s okay. I’m happy and want to carry on on this journey.”

Palestra has no doubt that the Premier League is the strongest and most competitive league in the world at the moment, and says that it is encouraging to see so many young Italians getting opportunities in England.

“In my opinion, it’s the best league in the world, the most competitive. The intensity is very high in England, there’s a lot of physicality but lots of quality as well. So I think it’s the best league in the world at the moment. Aside from the rivalries, it’s nice that there are so many young Italians playing in the Premier League, I’m happy about that.”

Palestra was introduced off the bench during Italy’s World Cup play-off final heartbreak against Bosnia earlier this year, which cost the Azzurri a place at the 2026 World Cup in North America.

“It still hurts a lot and I think it always will,” Palestra admitted. “But I know that we need to turn the page. We have to try to do that. What happened will always be there, but it’s up to us now to write great new pages in the history books of Italian sport.

“I always watch back all the games I play in. It was tough, but the only thing I can assure you is that we all gave our best to try and achieve that goal. It went the way it did and now it’s up to us to turn the page.”

Looking ahead to Thursday’s test against France, Palestra is looking forward to the possibility of testing himself against some of the finest attackers in the world: Kylian Mbappe, Ousmane Dembele, Desire Doue and Bradley Barcola are among the star names in the France squad.

“They’re some of the strongest players in the world and that is motivation for us. We have to be aware of our abilities and be ready,” said Palestra.

And on his start to life under Mancini, Palestra added: “From what I’ve seen so far, the feeling is positive. I obviously watched all the games that my teammates played in the last international breaks and it feels like a good group. We’re all united, the coach is great and helps us a lot, so, only positive feelings.”

Peter Young is a Senior Reporter with Football Italia.

XRP’s Quantum-Resistant Design Comes Into Focus

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » XRP’s Quantum-Resistant Design Comes Into Focus

The foundation behind XRP Ledger aims to mitigate quantum resistance hazards ahead of the practical test.

The chain developers behind Ripple’s native XRP Ledger are actively preparing to implement quantum-resistant measures. For this reason, the devs picked ML-DSA-44, a digital signature standard that the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) approved in 2024.

Earlier this year, Ripple introduced a multi-phase roadmap to prepare the XRP Ledger (XRPL) for a post-quantum future, with a target for full readiness by 2028. The new signature format needs 3,732 bytes for public keys and signatures, raising transaction data requirements. This will be audited in mid 2027.

JUST IN: $XRP Ledger unveils quantum-resistant account plan, allowing users to upgrade to quantum-proof security with no additional transaction fees as Ripple targets full quantum protection by 2028. pic.twitter.com/wS2Mk46nG8

As described in Google’s quantum computing research paper, there’s a few key risks the crypto sphere has to handle. The most quoted one is the exposable public key risks when users transact within the blockchain, something that quantum-computing based hackers could take serious advantage of.

There’s a more subtle risk too, one elliptic curve researchers call “harvest now, decrypt later.” Simply put, bad actors can collect publicly visible cryptographic data from the blockchain today and hold onto it. This raises the chances of the account eventually being broken into.

🚨 MASSIVE: THE $XRP LEDGER IS PREPARING FOR QUANTUM COMPUTERS. HERE IS WHAT NOBODY IS TELLING YOU.Every payment you sign reveals your public key.A powerful enough quantum computer could work backward from it.Any account that has ever sent a payment is already exposed.The… https://t.co/hpCLy5Gzzi pic.twitter.com/L32SemqQcE

The extra XRP network storage needed for this kind of upgrade doesn’t necessarily raise the fees. Node operators are absorbing the ~3.6 KB increase per protected transaction (from 105 bytes to 3,732 bytes for key plus the signature), as documented in the proposal. With database overhead, every secure payment would use 5,331 bytes of storage.

David Schwartz Explains What Could Really Drive XRP's ValueFormer Ripple (@Ripple) CTO David Schwartz (@JoelKatz) says using $XRP to pay XRPL (@XRP) gas fees is unlikely to drive its price significantly.He argues that broader network adoption could create value for XRP… pic.twitter.com/X0et9M7El9

The XRP Ledger foundation is planning the inaugural phase of the quantum resistance study in early 2027 as devs polish the proposition of key generation between the Python, JavaScript and server versions. Mitigations are done within a zero-knowledge proof to validate results without disclosing vectors.

Quantum physics may be a tough nut to crack for most crypto currencies, but XRP’s developers are set on a mission to get the chain shielded by 2028.

Microsoft awards $20K grants to 25 Seattle-area nonprofits amid state budget crunch and tax debate

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 10, 2026 at 10:48 amNovember 10, 2026 at 12:58 pm

The Lighthouse for the Blind is adding braille displays to its computer lab. Friends of the Issaquah Salmon Hatchery is taking its lessons to low-income schools. And Lake Washington High School’s band boosters are replacing marching capes that date to the MS-DOS era.

Those are among 25 King County nonprofits that received $20,000 apiece from Microsoft in grants announced this week. It’s the first round of the company’s Community-First Grants, a $1 million program launched in September.

Microsoft said it received 813 applications.

“Boy, I think we hit a nerve,” said Jane Broom, Microsoft’s senior director of Community Affairs, during an event Monday recognizing the nonprofits on the company’s Redmond campus, featuring Mariners catcher Cal Raleigh and the team’s mascots.

The grants are meant for immediate needs, such as replacing a broken-down van or a failed freezer, Broom explained. Nonprofits that already received or were invited to receive Microsoft corporate grants in 2026 weren’t eligible.

Funding and the tax debate: Nonprofits are dealing with rising costs and tighter budgets. As a group, their largest funding source is the state government, which is in the middle of an ongoing debate with members of the business community over taxes and economic competitiveness amid a budget crunch.

Microsoft is among the companies asserting that current and proposed taxes are hurting the business climate. It’s also the second-largest funder of the state’s nonprofits, behind only the state itself, said Brad Smith, the company’s president and vice chair.

“In my view, a healthy nonprofit community actually has aspects of support from more than one source,” Smith said in an interview after the event, noting that state grants are often at risk when a recession forces budget cuts.

Where the money goes: Demand is climbing for many of the grant recipients. At Community Lunch on Capitol Hill, which serves free hot meals, it’s up 40% this year, and the diaper bank at Byrd Barr Place has grown from 720 families in 2021 to 1,600, Microsoft said at the event.

Friends of the Issaquah Salmon Hatchery gives more than 700 tours during the salmon season. It will use its grant to bring lessons to classrooms, mainly at Title I schools, which serve large numbers of low-income students and often can’t afford a field trip to the hatchery, said Mark Clemens, the group’s executive director.

“If they can’t bring the school to the hatchery, we will go out to the school,” said Clemens, a former Scientific American art director, who was creative director of the Modernist Cuisine cookbook project before taking over the nonprofit in 2023.

One bonus for the group, which goes by the clever acronym of FISH, was the appearance of the Mariners’ salmon mascots, which joined the group onstage for a photo op when Clemens and his colleagues accepted its grant.

The Lighthouse for the Blind, a Seattle-based nonprofit that employs blind people in manufacturing, retail and other jobs, will use its grant to upgrade its computer training lab with Windows 11 PCs, braille displays and wireless keyboards, said Antonio Rozier, the nonprofit’s IT director.

The goal is to keep employees current on screen readers and other tools so they can work alongside sighted colleagues, Rozier said. The Lighthouse also has computer labs at its three manufacturing plants, and employees can use them for personal tasks, since some don’t have that equipment at home.

Rozier called the upgraded lab “a game changer for the future.”

The Lake Washington High School Band and Jazz Boosters will buy 150 uniforms for the school’s marching band, which returned in 2025 after a 40-year absence and has been performing in capes from 1981.

Microsoft’s company culture: Most of the company’s local giving is directed by its employees. Microsoft matches their donations and adds $25 for every hour they volunteer, up to a combined $15,000 a year per person, Smith said.

That program accounts for most of the $1.9 billion that Microsoft and its employees have given to about 13,000 Washington nonprofits over the past 30 years, he said.

“We let our people decide where the money goes,” Smith said.

Microsoft’s employee base is going through a period of major change. After layoffs in July and September, the company offered voluntary buyouts last month to about 60,000 people, roughly a quarter of its global workforce. Employees have described mounting pressure to use AI, stagnant pay and a growing sense of job insecurity, The New York Times reported.

Asked about the buyouts, Smith said it’s a good time for Microsoft to keep investing in the employee experience, and he described the giving program as part of that.

Silicon Valley companies may offer more free food as a perk, he said, but Microsoft matches employee donations, giving workers who often move to the region from elsewhere a way to get involved in the community.

This year’s Microsoft Give campaign in October, the company’s biggest month for employee donations, was again a success, Smith said, showing that “employee participation and employee giving is alive and well.”

Microsoft plans to open a second round of applications for the Community-First Grants in the spring of 2027.

We’re in a James Bond Fever Dream Now

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

“Next James Bond ‘REVEALED,’” reads a headline published by The Sun last week. “After a loose-lipped co-star blabs secret after ‘a few lagers.’” The article suggests that Josh O’Connor, who has recently been named in the wider press as a last-minute hopeful in the race to find the next Bond actor, is the shoe-in to be 007—all based on what one of O’Connor’s cast mates in the theater production Golden Boy might have maybe told a stranger while drunk at The French House in London.

“They said that he had confided in them about getting the role when the cast of Golden Boy had celebratory drinks after the play finished its run on Halloween,” the article continues. So, there it is: we have our next Bond! Source: someone told someone else down the pub. No, really, honest!

This is nothing against O’Connor, mind. As Ben Allen argued in the latest edition of GQ’s Intermission newsletter, he would make for a killer, surprising pick, suggestive of an “actually new direction” delineating from the hyper-machismo that defined Bond through to the end of the Craig era. His characters tend to be quieter and more introspective; his performance style is similarly subtle. (The hipster choice? Maybe, but obviously a great one.) But the article is a symptom of how utterly insane the maelstrom of reports around the next Bond have become as we seemingly enter the casting endgame, with screen tests reportedly taking place in October. Further, producer Amy Pascal has said that we can probably expect an announcement to come before the end of the year.

Over the past weekend, Robert Pattinson and Nicholas Hoult emerged out of nowhere as having taken part in those aforementioned screen tests. Both would’ve come as whiplash to anyone who has had a passing interest in the endless Bond casting conversation: sure, they’ve been in and around the pack over the years, but given neither are called “Callum Turner” or “Jack Lowden,” the only speculated names taken seriously for much of 2026, they’re big—but both welcome!— shocks. I’m as much of an RPatz supremacist as anyone else who likes to think they know ball about films, the actor routinely being the best thing about his projects, from Nolan’s Tenet and all of his zany arthouse movies to both The Odyssey and Primetime this year. Much love to our man Hoult too, an underrated cornerstone of franchise cinema writ large, from X-Men to the new Superman. But still: what the hell?

In their own article about Pattinson and Hoult joining the race, Variety might’ve actually buried the most baffling lede: according to them, Harry Styles has “had discussions about the part,” while they also note that The Gentlemen’s Theo James—long thought out of the running—is actually yet to be ruled out. No shade on Styles, but he hasn’t been in a film since he briefly dipped a toe in acting in 2022’s Don’t Worry Darling and My Policeman, neither of which exactly kickstarted his movie career. And, like, he’s Harry Styles. Bond song? Yeah, we can see the vision. But the idea of him actually being 007 feels more like the product of an ayahuasca fever dream.

Regardless, Styles’s suitability for the role isn’t really the point. What’s more insane is that the Bond casting conversation only seems to have hit a level of psychosis as it nears the finish line: every other hour, the reported frontrunner switches from Lowden to Turner to O’Connor or Pattinson, a fever pitch of speculation and rumor-mongering that was tired before it was tired. Gary Oldman reckons it should be Lowden! Pierce Brosnan backs Turner! As one person astutely put it on Threads: “OH MY GOD JUST PICK A JAMES BOND. DO ANY JAMES BOND, JUST JAMES BOND A JAMES BOND.”

But it’s not just Bond himself, it seems. As I sat down to write this piece, an email hit my inbox from the publicist for a betting company. Subject line: “Maya Jama odds slashed to star in the next Bond film.” Damn it. Just bring back Pierce.

This article originally appeared in British GQ.

Plaza Premium Opening Hong Kong Arrivals Lounge… With Live Music?!

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Plaza Premium will be opening an arrivals lounge soon at Hong Kong International Airport (HKG). While these facilities are always useful for a shower and breakfast after an overnight flight, it sounds like there might be a bit more planned for this arrivals lounge? I’m certainly intrigued (thanks to Sel_D for flagging this)…

Plaza Premium is expected to open an arrivals lounge at Hong Kong Airport in late November 2026. The lounge will be located on level three of Terminal 2, in the arrivals portion of the terminal, below the Airport Express platform. That means it’ll be landside.

This actually technically represents the reopening of a lounge, as the global lounge operator had such a facility until 2019. At that point it closed due to an expansion project at the airport. Once open, it will be the only arrivals lounge at the airport. Cathay Pacific used to have an arrivals lounge at its hub, but it closed in 2018, and the airline has no plans to reopen such a facility.

The exact entry requirements of the Plaza Premium arrivals lounge in Hong Kong remain to be seen, but like most Plaza Premium lounges, the expectation is that entry will be possible through select credit cards and Priority Pass, and access can also be purchased directly.

Generally we think of an arrivals lounge in terms of its functional purpose, which is to shower, have breakfast, and get some work done, before heading into the city. Many arrivals lounges are only open in the morning, since that’s when there’s the most value. That’s especially true for people who either plan to head to the office, or who are staying at a hotel, where it’s well before the published check-in time.

While it remains to be seen what exactly Plaza Premium’s facility will be like, it sounds like this will be a rather unconventional arrivals lounge, in a good way. The social media post about the new lounge describes it as “Plaza Premium Lounge – Rhythms.” It will offer a “seamless blend of dining, music, wellness, and refreshment.”

In terms of the range of amenities, we’re being promised live music, wellness experiences, refined dining, and private resting spaces. I’m sorry… live music? Like… someone performing music live? In an arrivals lounge? Fascinatingly, the pictures make it look more like an evening-oriented lounge serving cocktails and wine, for lounging around, rather than your typical arrivals lounge.

A post shared by Plaza Premium Lounge (@plazapremiumlounge)

Suffice it to say that I am very interested in what they come up with here. From a monetization standpoint, this seems kind of smart, since it could drum up more business in the afternoons and evenings, when people might not otherwise consider visiting such a facility. Then again, are people really thinking “I just got off a 15 hour flight, what I could really use is a cocktail before heading to my hotel?” Maybe they are, I suppose!

In the coming weeks, Hong Kong Airport will once again have an arrivals lounge, thanks to the opening of a facility by Plaza Premium. Arrivals lounges are usually pretty basic, and are about showers and breakfast. While we’ll see how much of this is fluff rather than actual substance, Plaza Premium is promising what sounds more like a bar vibe, with live music and cocktails. Bring it on, Plaza Premium, I can’t wait to learn more!

Study Tracks 3,000 Real-World Psychedelic Journeys

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Neuroscientists at the University of California, San Francisco (UCSF) are launching CANOPY, a global observational study designed to monitor up to 3,000 individuals taking psychedelics in real-world environments. Led by Dr. Robin Carhart-Harris, the initiative moves beyond artificial laboratory settings to evaluate naturalistic use across homes, ceremonies, and retreats.

Backed by up to $8.5 million in total ARPA-H funding across three translational UCSF projects, CANOPY combines longitudinal remote psychological tracking with in-person neuroimaging, EEG, biomaterial sampling, and wearable biometric monitoring to determine how set, setting, and biology shape therapeutic versus adverse outcomes.

Over the past decade, the resurgence of psychedelic medicine has yielded promising clinical trial data for treatment-resistant depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, and substance use disorders. Yet, the vast majority of human psychedelic use takes place far beyond the sterile walls of university medical centers, unfolding instead in private residences, underground therapeutic circles, ceremonial retreats, and festival environments.

Because clinical trials enforce rigid inclusion criteria, standardized dosing, and artificial hospital-room aesthetics, they leave a critical scientific blind spot: how do variable naturalistic settings, individual biological baselines, and subjective intentions influence long-term mental health outcomes?

To bridge this gap between clinical efficacy and real-world effectiveness, neuroscientists at the University of California, San Francisco (UCSF) have initiated CANOPY, a large-scale global observational study.

Funded by the federal Advanced Research Projects Agency for Health (ARPA-H)—an agency within the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services tasked with driving high-impact biomedical breakthroughs—the study will follow up to 3,000 people across the globe to map what psychedelics do to human brains, bodies, and long-term well-being.

CANOPY is led by Dr. Robin Carhart-Harris, PhD, the Ralph Metzner Distinguished Professor of Neurology and Psychiatry at UCSF and a pioneering figure in human psychedelic neuroimaging.

Unlike conventional clinical trials, the researchers do not supply, administer, or recommend any controlled substances. Instead, the study utilizes an observational, citizen-science design, enrolling individuals who have already made independent plans to consume psychedelic compounds.

The study encompasses a broad pharmacological spectrum, tracking users of classical 5-HT2A agonists (psilocybin, LSD, ayahuasca, mescaline), atypical entheogens (ibogaine), empathogens (MDMA), and dissociative anesthetics (ketamine).

“Psychedelics are widely used outside research labs, yet much of what we know about their effects comes from carefully controlled research settings,” said Carhart-Harris. “CANOPY gives us an opportunity to bring scientific rigor to these experiences at a scale we haven’t had before — to understand not just whether psychedelics can be beneficial, but for whom, under what circumstances, and why.”

The vast majority of participants will engage with the study remotely through decentralized digital platforms, completing validated psychological inventories before their planned session, during the immediate integration period, and longitudinally for up to six months afterward.

For participants able to travel to UCSF’s Neurosciences building in San Francisco, CANOPY incorporates deep biological phenotyping:

By combining granular physiological telemetry with longitudinal psychological endpoints, the team aims to parse out why identical doses can trigger transformative emotional healing in one individual and acute psychological distress or enduring anxiety in another.

CANOPY represents one branch of a tri-part, $8.5 million psychedelic research initiative awarded to UCSF by ARPA-H.

The broader initiative includes two parallel clinical projects:

As state, federal, and international jurisdictions reconsider the legal and therapeutic status of entheogens, real-world epidemiological safety data is urgently needed.

By analyzing the complex interactions among pharmacology, psychological vulnerability, social support, and somatic health across thousands of diverse naturalistic sessions, CANOPY will establish an open evidentiary baseline. The findings will assist clinicians in refining patient stratification criteria, guide harm-reduction protocols for non-clinical users, and direct the development of next-generation psychiatric trials.

What is the primary goal of UCSF’s CANOPY study?

CANOPY is an observational study tracking up to 3,000 adults worldwide who are already planning to take psychedelics in real-world settings (such as homes, retreats, or private ceremonies). The goal is to understand how these substances alter the brain, body, and long-term mental health outside of artificial laboratory environments.

Do UCSF researchers provide participants with psychedelics?

No. Researchers do not provide, prescribe, or encourage the acquisition of psychedelics. CANOPY is an observational, non-interventional trial enrolling people who have already scheduled their own personal sessions. The core study is conducted remotely via digital assessments.

What biological measurements are being collected?

In addition to remote surveys tracking mood and well-being for six months, a subset of participants visiting UCSF will undergo structural and functional MRI, EEG recordings, blood and saliva biomarker analysis, and continuous biometric monitoring using wearable health sensors.

United Airlines Executive Says Iconic Dulles Mobile Lounges Will Retire, But “Won’t Be Forgotten.” What Comes Next?

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

United Airlines wants Washington Dulles to move beyond its mobile lounges. But when I asked Andrew Nocella whether he actually likes them, his response suggested that retirement may not mean disappearance. Could one become a bar or café? Could a limited transportation role survive? His answer left room for some speculation.

During a press gaggle at Washington Dulles yesterday, I asked United Airlines Executive Vice President and Chief Commercial Officer Andrew Nocella a simple question: does he like the people movers? (it was in the context of their future place at an evolving IAD).

“I love it,” he responded, before explaining:

“The people movers, of course, is part of my history. I can remember, back in the day, coming out to the aircraft through the people mover, so it’s part of my history. But I do understand the need to evolve and change and innovate. And the people movers need to be innovated out. There are better ways to do it. But it will be an important part of the history of Dulles Airport and I can assure you that People Moves, while they will be retired, they won’t be forgotten.”

These vehicles are technically known as mobile lounges, though people movers is a fitting description too and I’ve called them “moon buggies” for years. Whatever you call them, I found that last assurance intriguing, even if we should be careful not to read too much into it.

Nocella did not announce a preservation project or describe a future use. But promising they will not be forgotten invites the question: how might Dulles preserve something so closely associated with the airport while finally moving beyond the need to use it?

My favorite possibility is turning one into a bar or café.

Imagine a retired mobile lounge parked in a suitable location, with its distinctive shape preserved and the interior converted into somewhere to have a drink while watching aircraft. Keep some of the original seating,  add photographs explaining its history, and suddenly an airport frustration becomes an attraction.

I would happily stop for a cappuccino. I suspect plenty of aviation enthusiasts would make a point of visiting too.

There would obviously be practical questions about access, utilities, and where to put it…maybe the wheels would have to be removed. A vehicle designed to transport passengers is not automatically suitable for operating a restaurant. But as a concept, it strikes me as a fun way to preserve a piece of Dulles without requiring anyone to ride it to immigration.

Nostalgia becomes much easier to appreciate when you can choose whether to participate!

Think how cool it would be to take one of these and put it inside the new Polaris Lounge in Concourse E that will be coming in the years ahead.

Another possibility is that the mobile lounges disappear from most journeys but continue in a narrower role.

I wonder, for example, whether there might be a useful way to run passengers between the D and E gates during the airport’s lengthy transition. Could a mobile lounge provide a convenient connection while the rest of the concourse development catches up? It woudl certainly save a lot of walking.

That is a question, not something Nocella proposed and I do realize that keeping an aging fleet operating for a limited purpose could undermine the savings and simplicity that retirement is supposed to deliver.

Still, “retired” can mean different things. Retired from routine passenger service would not necessarily preclude an occasional special ride or some other carefully limited use.

Of course, “won’t be forgotten” may mean something much less elaborate.

A preserved vehicle, a display in the terminal, or a design reference inside a future lounge could honor the mobile lounges without retaining their transportation function.

I would prefer preserving at least one actual vehicle rather than relying exclusively on photographs. These machines are unusual enough that future travelers should have an opportunity to see what generations of Dulles passengers experienced. I still love them and will always love them.

Nocella’s answer was affectionate, but clear about the need for change. He did not offer a retirement date or reveal what remembering the mobile lounges will look like.

But I do hope that assurance becomes something tangible. A mobile lounge café or bar would get my vote, preferably inside the new Polaris Lounge. A useful transitional shuttle or occasional heritage ride could be interesting too and much easier to maintain than a vintage aircraft.

For years, passengers have boarded these vehicles because they had to. It would be wonderful if Dulles eventually gave us a reason to step inside one because we wanted to.

What would you do with a retired Dulles mobile lounge?

Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

Aren’t we years from any potential retirement? This is just idle speculation when the future of Dulles isn’t even certain. The shiny new United Terminal will still use the people movers too.

I am daring myself BUT my first encounter with the mobile lounge coincided with United’s last DC-8 Super 71 service from SFO to IAD back in the early 1990’s. I enjoyed first class on United ” Ocean to Ocean Service” and the ” Moon Buggy” was an interesting experience as well.

James Gunn’s DC Universe Was Weird, Ambitious, and Ultimately Unfinished

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The writer-director is a casualty of Skydance’s acquisition of Warner Bros. He was building to a future that fans will now likely never see, for better or worse.

The hierarchy of power at DC Studios is changing again. On November 9, news broke that writer-director James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran left—later reported as ousted from—their jobs as co-CEOs and co-chairmen of DC Studios. Jeremy Latcham, a former Marvel Studios producer whose past credits include The Avengers and Gunn’s own Guardians of the Galaxy, will replace them on December 1.

Suddenly, DC fans woke up on a Monday to a new regime. But how will we look back on the four years that a prolific genre writer and director, who came up under Lloyd Kaufman’s B-movie studio Troma, oversaw an entire superhero universe?

It was a time of a lot of promise, not all of it fulfilled.

Before we start the eulogies for the DC Universe, it’s important to note that things aren’t over. Yet. Latcham will take over Gunn and Safran’s posts, so DC Studios is still up and running with more movies in the pipeline, many of which bear Gunn’s fingerprints. Man of Tomorrow, for example. The 2027 movie is a direct sequel to Superman, also written and directed by Gunn. Among its biggest moves is introducing a new Wonder Woman (rumored, but not confirmed, to be Adria Arjona). The Batman: Part II, the long-awaited sequel to Matt Reeves’s The Batman, will also open in 2028 with Gunn and Safran involved as producers. Gunn also said on Instagram they have been “engaged on another DC project which I’m writing.”

Both Gunn and Safran are expected to stay at DC, with Deadline confirming they will “continue to produce on various DC projects.” That said, Matthew Belloni at Puck published a story citing sources who say the DCU will reboot, again, after Man of Tomorrow. And at The Hollywood Reporter, Borys Kit and Aaron Crouch detailed the friction Gunn faced while at the studio, and how Gunn and Safran’s exits were forced by new Skydance leaders David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz. THR reports the DC heads felt secure after the big Warner-Paramount merger; Clayface had grossed $100 million, and Gunn was preparing a draft for the third Superman movie when they were told they were out. You can say the DCU is in a crisis right now.

For now, we can say that a critical chapter of DC is over. Gunn will no longer apply his vision and filter across all DC projects, which he must feel a little relieved about. By his own admission, which he wrote on Instagram on November 9, Gunn expressed feeling stretched thin, having to exert energy on movies and shows he wasn’t emotionally invested in to begin with. Said Gunn: “[T]he thing my ADHD hyperfocus has had the most difficulty with has been dividing my time between projects I’m incredibly passionate about and the ones I’m not so much and all the things I have to do as a studio head.”

Gunn and Safran got their jobs in late 2022, but it wasn’t until 2024 that people saw the first glimpse of their reign. Though Gunn had already worked for DC on 2021’s The Suicide Squad and created the spin-off Peacemaker, those projects were separate from other things at DC, then run by Walter Hamada. At the time, its biggest bet was Black Adam, Dwayne Johnson’s long-in-development antihero project that fell flat and led to nowhere. When Gunn took the reins of DC soon after, the former Marvel director had carte blanche to tailor the sandbox to his vision.

In January 2023, Gunn revealed his plans for DC Studios in something he called “Chapter 1: Gods and Monsters.” In a six-minute YouTube video, Gunn ran down his plans for the movies and shows that made up his master plan for the next decade of DC Studios. But for every mention of this movie or that HBO Max show, the first project carried significant weight. It was the first step, the real first impression, as a showcase of what Gunn would do with a DCU bent to his will. What icons would make it to the screen? What legend would get their spotlight? What iconic storylines might be retold on the big screen? What, really, will James Gunn’s DC become?

His first order of business was... Creature Commandos.

In fairness, Creature Commandos was a great show (if you saw it). It was an adult animated series where horror and pulp-inspired superheroes teamed up as a black ops strike force. While a show like that wouldn’t be anyone’s idea of a great introduction, Gunn’s background as the writer of movies like Tromeo & Juliet and his success with Guardians of the Galaxy for Marvel at least made sense. It basically said without ambiguity that this new DCU is being molded by no one else but Gunn.

Then, Superman premiered in summer 2025. Initially written by Gunn, the DC Studios head eventually named himself the director of DC’s first theatrical release under his stewardship. Despite my own misgivings about the movie, it was the reorientation a refreshed superhero universe needed. You simply can’t have a DC Universe without the Man of Steel, whose first appearance in Action Comics #1 in 1938 kicked off the modern superhero as we know it.

Superman made a solid first impression for DC at the cineplex. So there was plenty of optimism for the 2026 spin-off Supergirl starring Milly Alcock. Largely based on the acclaimed Tom King and Bilquis Evely graphic novel Supergirl: Woman of Tomorrow, the pseudo-sequel was poised to keep up DC’s newfound momentum. It didn’t. Supergirl hit a wall, much like Black Adam. No superhero universe is immune to duds, but given it was just DC’s second time at bat, a blemish on what still felt like a new, fresh-out-of-the-box DCU felt grave.

But one dud wasn’t enough to kneecap the DCU. The same summer saw the premiere of Lanterns, which fostered a faithful audience on HBO Sunday nights during its buzzy eight-episode first season. Despite mixed reviews, Clayface has grossed a profitable $100 million against a slim budget. Between the next Superman and Batman sequels, there was still plenty to look forward to. Until now.

James Gunn’s reign wasn’t impervious to bad news either. Since his January 2023 reveal, many of the projects have wound up on the back burner: The Authority, which had been a “passion project” for Gunn (with plenty of setup for it in Superman); Waller, a TV show with Viola Davis reprising her role from the Suicide Squad films; Paradise Lost, a political epic set in Wonder Woman’s home; and Booster Gold, a superhero comedy about a goofball from the future. And throughout his reign, Gunn put up with questions about his Batman (not Matt Reeves’s), which he would have introduced in The Brave and the Bold. Gunn sweated over the character so much that a cameo of Batman in Creature Commandos was shrouded in shadow, because he said he was still deciding on what the costume was going to look like.

Since the start of Gunn’s DC, it was clear his architecture came from a place of affection for the brand, and for very specific corners of it. His body of work reflected his scraggly upbringing as a punk creative, with empathy for characters who were too weird for their own Saturday morning cartoon. The Suicide Squad elevated such figures, like Weasel and Polka-Dot Man, whose climax involved a fight with Starro, a colossal evil starfish. Though his Superman was a sunny throwback to the Christopher Reeve classics, it was also jam-packed with the likes of Green Lantern, Mister Terrific, Metamorpho, and a villain Gunn was clearly setting up to be Bizarro.

Multiple times on social media, Gunn shared artwork from one DC book in particular: Kingdom Come, written by Mark Waid and illustrated by Alex Ross. Published in 1996, the story was a treatise of sorts by Waid, who lamented the state of superhero comics at the time. The story sees an older Superman who comes out of retirement to reform the Justice League, only to meet physical and philosophical resistance—predominantly from Batman.

Gunn’s allusions to Kingdom Come, including the exact shape of Superman’s “S” in his film, suggested he was laying the groundwork for a Kingdom Come-style event movie. It would have been an ambitious endeavor, oddly fitting to Gunn’s sensibilities. In the few years we saw Gunn at work for DC, he was building a universe where tried-and-true icons of superhero comics brushed up against the prickly and the bizarre. Where weirdness leaked from the orifices of a summer tentpole with an action figure set.

It often feels like Gunn was just getting started, but four years with two (soon three) movies written and directed by him, several TV shows—and Supergirl and Clayface, whatever you think of them—leave a big enough impression to suggest that Gunn’s DC had no shortage of imagination, even if his signature flourishes had grown familiar. Not every superhero film needs to have shock F-bombs and action scenes set to ironic licensed music, but Gunn had a lane. He excelled at staying in it.

It’s doubtful Gunn and Safran (who took on the business end of things) will stay on at DC under Skydance leadership for long. They will probably see Man of Tomorrow and The Batman: Part II through. If those movies do well, they might stick around to keep making movies, because for the most part, they have a magic that works. If the new movies fail to take off, then Gunn might finally find new stories to tell. It’s hard to say which path is the better one.

Gunn’s Kingdom Come, whatever it might have been, pointed to a future that never became a promise. In his farewell message, Gunn wrote that “the characters we set up will live on.” Whether the next era will let the DCU stay weird is another matter.

Company of Heroes 3 Announces Mac Version

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Michael Manning (He/Him) has been enamored with video games since he first played the Sonic Classic Collection as a child. With decades of experience in gaming and a full tour as a Gaming Journalist, Michael is ready to step up to the plate. From AAA to the smallest passion projects, his motto is that every piece of ar

Many outside of the PC gaming sphere view it as a single homogenized entity centered around Windows, and while plenty of users are focused on Microsoft’s OS, others are shafted by certain releases. Whether it’s Linux or Mac, Windows being the central platform means plenty of releases just miss the alternate PC operating systems, leaving them out to dry when they’d love to join.

Wuthering Waves is one of the biggest Gachas on the planet, and they do far better than most at answering player complaints. Let's go over exactly how!

Relic Entertainment has announced in a YouTube video that its military tactics game Company of Heroes 3 is getting an official macOS port in December, with no exact date specified. Apple-bound players will finally get their chance to play the latest title in the prestigious Company of Heroes series.

World War 2 has been a fixture of gaming practically as long as it has existed, and with its prevalence throughout hundreds of titles, new entries have stepped up to showcase theatres of war outside of Eastern Europe. Company of Heroes 3 makes its mark by focusing on the Italian and North African campaigns, two less glamorous theatres of war that have long earned the spotlight.

The macOS version of Company of Heroes 3 is also launching with the major shake-ups of all previous balance and content patches, including the 3.0 overhaul, but it appears all DLC will need to be purchased separately, one of the biggest criticisms of the game’s current Steam release. To celebrate, the game is currently on sale on Steam as well, giving curious players a chance to hop in as the potential player base grows.

Mac players will also be able to nab the standalone Final Stand mode, a survival battle using most of Company of Heroes 3’s systems to simulate an endless onslaught of attackers. The mode itself has also launched to mixed reviews, as players seem rather tired of the DLC practices of the game itself. The macOS version also requiring the DLC feels like a missed opportunity to win back some fans.

Whether it’s Linux or Mac, Windows being the expectation and central platform means plenty of releases just miss the alternate PC operating systems, leaving them out to dry when they’d love to join.

All in all, the port is exactly what it says on the tin, a chance for macOS fans of the franchise to finally jump on the 2023 release, almost 4 years after its initial drop. Anyone still loyal to the series who hasn’t gotten the chance to play it is likely chomping at the bit and can only hope Relic implements a sale when it finally does make the leap over.

Relic Entertainment has been in a bit of a rough spot as of late, after being passed over as the team for the newest Dawn of War title. Dawn of War, the real-time Warhammer 40K tactics series, has long since been Relic’s baby alongside Company of Heroes, and seeing them off the project is a massive shake-up.

For now, running off this port and last year’s Dawn of War 2 remaster, Relic Entertainment is looking for some small wins to get themselves back in the game, something this new macOS port should really help with. Opening their latest titles to fresh faces should lead to a big boost both in revenue and players, something they desperately need as they move onto their next project.

Relic is stacking up an easy win with the upcoming port of Company of Heroes 3, one of their stronger titles. Fans know the DLC song and dance and are far more willing to play ball, but Relic needs newcomers now more than ever.

Take-Two has had an absolutely killer Q2, leading them to feed GTA fever more and more.

Juventus star Kenan Yildiz expects to be back on the pitch next month, having been out of action since August after fracturing his metatarsal.

Yildiz enjoyed a breakout campaign in 2025-26 despite Juve missing out on Champions League qualification on the final matchday, scoring 10 goals and assisting six in Serie A.

He was one of just four players born in 2005 or later to register 15 goal involvements in one of Europe's top five leagues last term, alongside Lamine Yamal (27), Yan Diomande (20) and Said El Mala (17).

However, the Turkiye international has only appeared once in 2026-27, versus Frosinone on August 23, before being forced to undergo surgery on his left foot later that month.

In his absence, Juve have endured a frustrating start to their first full season under Luciano Spalletti, sitting seventh in Serie A with 18 points from 11 matches, following Sunday's 2-2 draw with Fiorentina.

But the cavalry is coming, with Yildiz not expecting to miss too many more games after the international break.

McTominay reveals he underwent heart treatment two days before World Cup

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Scott McTominay has revealed he had to receive treatment for an irregular heartbeat before Scotland's opening game at the World Cup in June.

McTominay recently returned to action with Napoli, after being diagnosed with a "mild benign arrhythmia" and undergoing a minor procedure to correct the condition.

The former Manchester United midfielder subsequently missed the first four games of Scotland's Nations League Group B1 campaign, from which they took four points.

He could feature under the Tartan Army's new head coach Sebastien Pocognoli for the first time this month, having returned to the squad for their games against North Macedonia and Switzerland.

And ahead of Friday's meeting with North Macedonia at Hampden Park, McTominay has revealed his heart condition began affecting him five months ago.

"I had to go and get the shock on my heart. It was two days before the Haiti game," McTominay – who then played the full 90 minutes as Scotland started their World Cup campaign with a 1-0 win – said.

"You've prepared so well. I was in such a good condition and then something like that happened. You're thinking, 'how is this happening to me?'

"You have to get on with it; those are the cards that you're dealt in football. Sometimes, things don't go your way.

"I was 100%, and obviously, when you're sat in a hospital bed two days before the first game of the World Cup, about to get put to sleep, it's not ideal, is it?"

McTominay was one of four players to play every minute for Scotland at the World Cup, though he failed to score or assist at the tournament, only creating one chance and hitting the target with three of his eight shots.

He has scored five goals for Napoli since his return to action, with three coming in the Champions League and the other two being a brace in a thrilling 3-3 draw with Roma.

And he now feels back to something approaching his best, saying: "I'm feeling much, much better than I was.

"It was something that needed to be done. In your career, you have moments like that where things can take you by surprise.

"Obviously, it looks a lot worse to people in the media, and family and friends reach out to you after they see news related to a heart issue, but it's a relatively straightforward procedure, which went really, really well in Manchester.

"So, I'm just really happy to be back and playing football again. That's the most important thing in my life and it always will be."

They have not lost three in a row on their own turf since September 2018, while in competitive games they have never previously lost three straight home fixtures.

Injective Price Prediction 2026, 2027–2030: Will INJ Price Reclaim $15?
